### Sub-requirements

#### 9.1.1 — Controller-side data-protection policies with DPO governance anchor and Art. 5(2) accountability

```yaml
- req_id: 9.1.1
  volere_section: 15
  volere_subtype: integrity

  title: "Controller implements appropriate data-protection policies reviewed and updated where necessary, with DPO designation, independence, and Art. 5(2) accountability"

  derives_from:
    hso_sub_so: SO-9.1.GDPR
    per_reg_so: SO-GDPR-026 + SO-GDPR-027 + SO-GDPR-037
    source_sr: SR-GDPR-043 + SR-GDPR-044 + SR-GDPR-063
    hso_file: ../../HierarchicalSecurityObjectives/D-09_Governance-Documentation/9.1.md
    objective_ref_section: "### 9.1.1"

  description: |
    The product shall establish, document, review, and update appropriate data-protection policies designed to implement data-protection principles in an effective manner (per GDPR Art. 24(1) + Art. 24(2)), with the DPO designated per Art. 37(1) triggers and operating with the independence, resources, and expert-knowledge maintenance required by Art. 38 (per Art. 38(1) timely involvement + Art. 38(2) resources + Art. 38(3) direct reporting + Art. 38(6) conflict-of-interests prohibition), and with Art. 5(2) accountability burden demonstrating continuous compliance with the Art. 5(1) principles.

  fit_criterion: |
    Appropriate data-protection policies are documented and enforced under Art. 24(1) `appropriate technical and organisational measures, such as policies … which are designed to implement data-protection principles … in an effective manner` (SR-GDPR-043) with GV.PO-01 policy-instrument discipline (every controller has a communicated, current, enforced cybersecurity-and-data-protection policy anchored on the Art. 24(2) proportionality test) + GV.PO-02 operational-procedure layer (each policy backed by documented processes — incident response, breach notification, data-subject rights, retention, processor onboarding — so the policy is real rather than paper) + GV.RM-04 strategic-direction and review-coupling (the controller communicates how it identifies and responds to risks, including the cadence and triggers for policy and procedure refresh, operationalising the Art. 24(1) `where necessary` review obligation on a material-change-of-risk trigger supplemented by annual periodic review per EDPB Guidelines 07/2019 on accountability); the policies are reviewed and updated `where necessary` against the material-change-of-risk trigger; the controller can demonstrate continuous compliance under Art. 5(2) accountability (SR-GDPR-044) with GV.PO-02 procedural-evidence backbone (RoPA entries, DPIA artefacts, audit logs, training records, breach-response records preserved on a continuous basis per EDPB Guidelines on accountability 07/2019) + GV.OV-03 evaluated-and-reviewed-correction dimension (controller's performance against cybersecurity and data-protection objectives measured and adjusted, with adjustments materialising as refreshed policies, processes, and procedures); a DPO is designated where any of the Art. 37(1) triggers applies (public authority + core-activity regular/systematic monitoring of data subjects on large scale + core-activity large-scale special-category or criminal-conviction processing), with Art. 37(7) publication of the designation (SR-GDPR-062), and operates with Art. 38 independence and resourcing (SR-GDPR-063): timely involvement in all Art. 38(1) issues relating to personal-data protection + Art. 38(2) resources necessary to carry out Art. 39 tasks + access to personal data and processing operations + expert-knowledge maintenance + Art. 38(3) direct reporting to highest management level with no instructions regarding exercise of Art. 39 tasks + Art. 38(4) protection against dismissal or penalty for performing tasks + Art. 38(6) conflict-of-interests prohibition (DPO may not hold roles with direct data-processing decision authority — head of marketing, head of IT operations, CISO where that role includes decisions about processing purposes or means — per EDPB Guidelines 3/2018 §6); the DPO performs the Art. 39(1) tasks with adequate independence, resources, and expert knowledge (advisory, monitoring, assurance); for an integrated controller-NSIE, the NIS 2 management-body approve/oversee/liable governance architecture extends to the GDPR data-protection policies on the GDPR↔NIS2 SAME — COMPLEMENTARY pair (NIS 2 management-body approval minute as governance hook on top of the GDPR policy artefact — policies are stacked not collapsed, per CRDA-deep §9.1 GDPR↔NIS2 verified relationship); for a DORA financial entity that is a controller, the DORA Art. 6(1) ICT risk-management framework is the carrier and the GDPR Art. 24(1) data-protection policies are a module within the framework on the GDPR↔DORA SAME — COMPLEMENTARY pair (DORA-dominant for financial entities, per CRDA-deep §9.1 GDPR↔DORA verified relationship); for a provider/deployer of high-risk AI that is also a controller, the AI Act QMS becomes a sub-policy of the GDPR data-protection policies on the GDPR↔AI_Act SAME — COMPLEMENTARY pair (AI Act Recital 78 explicit GDPR preservation, per CRDA-deep §9.1 GDPR↔AI_Act verified relationship); verified by Art. 24(1) data-protection policy documentation review + Art. 24(1) `where necessary` review-cadence records + Art. 5(2) accountability evidence pack (RoPA + DPIA + audit logs + training records + breach-response records) + Art. 37(7) DPO designation publication + Art. 38(3) direct-reporting documentation + Art. 38(6) conflict-of-interests assessment + DPA supervisory review on request.

  priority: MUST
  verification_method: TEST
  dependencies: [9.1.2 (when controller = NSIE — NIS 2 management-body approval minute as governance hook on top of GDPR data-protection policies; policies stacked, not collapsed), 9.1.4 (when DORA-controller — DORA Art. 6(1) ICT risk-management framework as carrier + GDPR Art. 24(1) data-protection policies as module; DORA-dominant stack), 9.1.5 (when provider/deployer = controller — AI Act QMS as sub-policy of GDPR data-protection policies; AI Act Recital 78 explicit GDPR preservation)]

  nist_csf: [GV.PO-01, GV.PO-02, GV.RM-04, GV.RR-01, GV.RR-02, GV.RR-03, GV.OV-03]

  applicable_if:
    regs: [GDPR]
    scope_overlap: Conditional   # GDPR↔NIS2 = Y (controller-NSIE; NIS 2 management-body approve/oversee/liable governance extends to GDPR data-protection policies); GDPR↔CRA = Conditional (Y when controller is also manufacturer or OSS steward — different artefacts, different actors); GDPR↔DORA = Y (DORA financial entity = controller; DORA framework as carrier + GDPR data-protection policies as module; DORA-dominant for financial entities); GDPR↔AI_Act = Y (provider/deployer = controller; AI Act QMS as sub-policy of GDPR data-protection policies; AI Act Recital 78 explicit GDPR preservation); HL engages on overlap

  satisfaction:
    controls: []
    nodes: []

  considerations: |
    The GDPR information-security-policy obligation at 9.1 is the **controller-side data-protection policies** with **DPO as governance anchor** under Art. 24(1) + Art. 24(2) + Art. 5(2) accountability + DPO designation per Art. 37-39. Art. 24(1) `appropriate technical and organisational measures, such as policies … which are designed to implement data-protection principles … in an effective manner` + Art. 24(2) `appropriate data-protection policies` (the OJ's `such as` formulation admits a broader operational-measure cluster but `data-protection policies` are the named 9.1 anchor); Art. 5(2) accountability + Art. 30(4) RoPA (cross to 9.4) + Art. 35(7) DPIA content (cross to 9.2). The `where necessary` review cadence (Art. 24(1)) carries Berry-flagged VAG-S3 (Berry §5.1); reading adopted: material-change-of-risk trigger supplemented by annual periodic review (EDPB Guidelines 07/2019 on accountability). The DPO designation under Art. 37(1) fires on any of the three triggers (public authority + core-activity regular/systematic monitoring on large scale + core-activity large-scale special-category or criminal-conviction processing); the `core activities` (VAG-S3), `regular and systematic` + `large scale` (VAG-S3 + POLY-S3), and Berry-flagged `nature, scope and/or purposes` operator — chosen readings per EDPB Guidelines 3/2018 §3.1. The DPO operates with Art. 38(1)-(6) independence (timely involvement + resources + direct reporting + no instructions + protection against dismissal/penalty + conflict-of-interests prohibition); per EDPB Guidelines 3/2018 §6, the DPO cannot also hold roles with direct data-processing decision authority (head of marketing, head of IT operations, CISO where that role includes decisions about processing purposes or means). The recipient is the **DPA (supervisory authority)** on Art. 5(2) accountability + Art. 24(1) appropriate-measures demonstration + Art. 30(4) RoPA + Art. 35(7) DPIA content + DPO designation records. For an integrated controller-NSIE, the NIS 2 management-body approve/oversee/liable governance extends to the GDPR data-protection policies on the GDPR↔NIS2 SAME — COMPLEMENTARY pair (per CRDA-deep §9.1 GDPR↔NIS2 verified relationship). For a DORA financial entity that is a controller, the DORA Art. 6(1) ICT risk-management framework is the carrier and the GDPR data-protection policies are a module within the framework on the GDPR↔DORA SAME — COMPLEMENTARY pair (DORA-dominant for financial entities). For a provider/deployer of high-risk AI that is a controller, the AI Act QMS becomes a sub-policy of the GDPR data-protection policies on the GDPR↔AI_Act SAME — COMPLEMENTARY pair (AI Act Recital 78 explicit GDPR preservation). (Copied from the HSO Considerations block of `### 9.1.1`; preserved here for self-contained traceability.)
```

#### 9.1.2 — Entity-level risk-analysis + IS-security + effectiveness-assessment policy artefacts with management-body approve/oversee/liable architecture

```yaml
- req_id: 9.1.2
  volere_section: 15
  volere_subtype: integrity

  title: "Essential and important entities establish policies on risk analysis, information system security, and effectiveness assessment, approved and overseen by the management body"

  derives_from:
    hso_sub_so: SO-9.1.NIS2
    per_reg_so: SO-NIS2-021 + SO-NIS2-022 + SO-NIS2-023
    source_sr: SR-NIS2-032 + SR-NIS2-035 + SR-NIS2-036
    hso_file: ../../HierarchicalSecurityObjectives/D-09_Governance-Documentation/9.1.md
    objective_ref_section: "### 9.1.2"

  description: |
    The product shall establish and maintain documented policies on risk analysis and information system security (per NIS 2 Art. 21(2)(a)) and policies and procedures to assess the effectiveness of cybersecurity risk-management measures (per NIS 2 Art. 21(2)(f)), with the management body approving the Art. 21 measures and overseeing their implementation (per Art. 20(1) sentence 1 first-clause `approve` + second-clause `oversees`) and bearing personal liability for infringements (per Art. 20(1) sentence 1 third-clause `can be held liable`).

  fit_criterion: |
    Documented policies are established and maintained under Art. 21(2)(a) `policies on risk analysis and information system security` (SR-NIS2-035) documenting the entity's approach to identifying, assessing, and treating cybersecurity risks to network and information systems, and under Art. 21(2)(f) `policies and procedures to assess the effectiveness of cybersecurity risk-management measures` (SR-NIS2-036) with results feeding back into the risk-management cycle, with the depth calibrated by Art. 21(1) `appropriate and proportionate` chapeau + state-of-the-art floor (Art. 21(1) sentence 2) + Recital 56 proportionality (nature/size/risk profile/cost of implementation); the management body formally approves the Art. 21 measures through a documented approval minute (SR-NIS2-032) with GV.RR-02 approval as documented governance act (the approval minute records the management body's reading of the measures, acceptance of residual risk, and resource commitment, creating executive sponsorship) + GV.PO-01 downstream enforceability (the approved measures become the binding organisational cybersecurity policy); the management body continuously oversees the implementation (SR-NIS2-033) with GV.OV-01 active-monitoring dimension (management body reviews implementation status, receives exception reports, interrogates the CISO on residual-risk drift, authorises adjustments to the Art. 21 measures as the threat landscape and entity exposure evolve) + GV.RM-05 upward-channel substrate (the CISO and relevant functions have documented escalation paths to the management body, including exception and incident reporting); the management body bears personal liability for Art. 21 infringements (SR-NIS2-034) under Art. 20(1) sentence 1 third-clause `can be held liable` — the literacy prerequisite is the Art. 20(2) sentence 1 training obligation (cross-ref 8.3.1) which makes the management body structurally positioned to discharge Art. 20(1) oversight responsibilities with informed judgement; the policy artefact is layered under Art. 21(1) chapeau with the three policy records (Art. 21(2)(a) risk-analysis + IS-security policies + Art. 21(2)(f) effectiveness-assessment policies + procedures) and the management-body approval minute as governance hook; the policy content is entity-discretionary under Recital 56 proportionality — the OJ does not enumerate specific content; for an integrated controller-NSIE, the NIS 2 management-body approve/oversee/liable governance architecture extends to the GDPR data-protection policies on the GDPR↔NIS2 SAME — COMPLEMENTARY pair (NIS 2 management-body approval minute as governance hook on top of the GDPR policy artefact — policies are stacked not collapsed, per CRDA-deep §9.1 GDPR↔NIS2 verified relationship); for a DORA financial entity that is also an NSIE, the DORA Art. 6(1) ICT risk-management framework discharges both DORA's dedicated framework obligation and NIS 2's Art. 21(2)(a)+(f) policy artefacts + Art. 20(1) governance act on the NIS 2 ↔ DORA SAME — EQUAL pair (DORA-dominant stack for financial entities, per CRDA-deep §9.1 NIS2↔DORA verified relationship); for an NSIE that deploys high-risk AI, the AI Act QMS becomes a sub-policy of the entity-level NIS 2 governance framework on the NIS2↔AI_Act SAME — COMPLEMENTARY pair (the AI Act layer adds the AI-specific 13-element QMS to the NIS 2 risk-analysis policy, per CRDA-deep §9.1 NIS2↔AI_Act verified relationship); for an entity that procures CRA-conformant products, the CRA presumption-of-conformity machinery substitutes for parts of the NIS 2 supplier-assessment evidence obligation (cross to 6.1) but does not substitute for the NIS 2 entity-level policy architecture on the NIS2↔CRA SAME — COMPLEMENTARY pair (Conditional Y when entity procures CRA-conformant products, per CRDA-deep §9.1 NIS2↔CRA verified relationship); verified by Art. 21(2)(a) + Art. 21(2)(f) policy-records review + Art. 20(1) management-body approval minute + Art. 20(1) oversight records (board minutes + dashboard reviews + exception registers) + Art. 21(1) chapeau documented-policies evidence + Member State supervisory review (national NIS 2 supervisory authority) on request.

  priority: MUST
  verification_method: TEST
  dependencies: [8.3.1 (Art. 20(2) sentence 1 management-body training as literacy prerequisite for Art. 20(1) approve/oversee/liable), 9.1.1 (when controller-NSIE — NIS 2 management-body approval minute as governance hook on top of GDPR data-protection policies; policies stacked, not collapsed), 9.1.4 (when DORA-NSIE — DORA Art. 6(1) ICT risk-management framework discharges both DORA's dedicated framework obligation and NIS 2's Art. 21(2)(a)+(f) policy artefacts + Art. 20(1) governance act; EQUAL classification, DORA-dominant stack for financial entities), 9.1.5 (when NSIE deploys high-risk AI — AI Act QMS as sub-policy of NIS 2 entity-level governance), 9.1.3 (when entity procures CRA-conformant — CRA presumption substitutes for parts of NIS 2 supplier-assessment evidence but not NIS 2 entity-level policy architecture)]

  nist_csf: [GV.PO-01, GV.RR-02, GV.OV-01, GV.RM-05]

  applicable_if:
    regs: [NIS2]
    scope_overlap: Conditional   # NIS2↔GDPR = Y (controller-NSIE; NIS 2 management-body approve/oversee/liable governance extends to GDPR data-protection policies); NIS2↔CRA = Conditional (Y when entity procures CRA-conformant products); NIS2↔DORA = Y (DORA financial entity = NSIE; DORA-dominant stack for financial entities — EQUAL on policy + governance dimension); NIS2↔AI_Act = Y (NSIE deploys high-risk AI; AI Act QMS as sub-policy of NIS 2 entity-level governance); HL engages on overlap

  satisfaction:
    controls: []
    nodes: []

  considerations: |
    The NIS 2 information-security-policy obligation at 9.1 is the **entity-level policy artefacts** (Art. 21(2)(a) risk-analysis + IS-security policies + Art. 21(2)(f) effectiveness-assessment policies and procedures) with the **management-body approve/oversee/liable architecture** as governance hook (Art. 20(1) sentence 1 three-verb coordination `approve, oversee, and can be held liable` — three distinct obligations per Recital 75, per SR-NIS2-032/033/034 ambiguity readings). The `cybersecurity risk-management measures` (SR-NIS2-032) carry an S3 ambiguity on the approval object — an Art. 21(2)(a)-(j) sub-point list reading, an Art. 21(1) chapeau-and-Art. 21(5) implementing-act reading, or a free-standing entity-cybersecurity-strategy reading — chosen reading: literal-and-broad interpretation that `measures` covers both the Art. 21(2) sub-points and any entity-discretionary measures adopted under Art. 21(1). The `in order to comply with Article 21` qualifier carries an indicative-vs-restrictive ambiguity on whether the approval is for Art. 21 measures specifically or for any measures contributing to compliance — indicative reading dominant since the OJ uses the qualifier to anchor the approval object rather than constrain it. The `oversees` obligation is continuous by construction (no terminal-event qualifier) — the management body reviews implementation status and adjusts as needed on entity-discretionary cadence. The three-verb S3 COORD ambiguity (`approve, oversee, and can be held liable`) — chosen reading: three-distinct-obligations reading per Recital 75. Member State transposition divergence may apply on documentation expected to evidence oversight (board minutes, dashboard reviews, exception registers). The policy content is entity-discretionary under Recital 56 proportionality — OJ does not enumerate specific content. The recipient is the **management body + competent authority (national NIS 2 supervisory authority)** on Art. 21(2)(a)+(f) policy records + Art. 21(1) documented policies + Art. 20(1) management-body approval + oversight records. For an integrated controller-NSIE, the NIS 2 management-body approve/oversee/liable governance extends to the GDPR data-protection policies on the GDPR↔NIS2 SAME — COMPLEMENTARY pair. For a DORA financial entity that is also an NSIE, the DORA Art. 6(1) ICT risk-management framework discharges both DORA's dedicated framework obligation and NIS 2's Art. 21(2)(a)+(f) policy artefacts + Art. 20(1) governance act on the NIS 2 ↔ DORA SAME — EQUAL pair (DORA-dominant stack for financial entities). For an NSIE that deploys high-risk AI, the AI Act QMS becomes a sub-policy of the entity-level NIS 2 governance framework on the NIS2↔AI_Act SAME — COMPLEMENTARY pair. For an entity that procures CRA-conformant products, the CRA presumption-of-conformity machinery substitutes for parts of the NIS 2 supplier-assessment evidence obligation (cross to 6.1) but does not substitute for the NIS 2 entity-level policy architecture on the NIS2↔CRA SAME — COMPLEMENTARY pair. (Copied from the HSO Considerations block of `### 9.1.2`; preserved here for self-contained traceability.)
```

#### 9.1.3 — Manufacturer / OSS-steward policies with presumption-of-conformity machinery via harmonised standards / common specifications / CSA certification

```yaml
- req_id: 9.1.3
  volere_section: 15
  volere_subtype: integrity

  title: "Manufacturer and OSS-steward cybersecurity policies with verifiable documentation, CVD, support-period, and presumption of conformity via harmonised standards, common specifications, or CSA certification"

  derives_from:
    hso_sub_so: SO-9.1.CRA
    per_reg_so: SO-CRA-049 + SO-CRA-050 + SO-CRA-051 + SO-CRA-052
    source_sr: SR-CRA-072 + SR-CRA-073 + SR-CRA-075 + SR-CRA-077
    hso_file: ../../HierarchicalSecurityObjectives/D-09_Governance-Documentation/9.1.md
    objective_ref_section: "### 9.1.3"

  description: |
    The product shall establish manufacturer policies/processes/procedures necessary to implement CRA cybersecurity obligations — including the CVD policy under Annex I Part II §5 + Art. 13(8) sentence 6, the secure-update distribution mechanism under Annex I Part II §7, the support-period determination methodology under Art. 13(8) sentence 2, and the cessation-of-operations communication procedure under Art. 13(23) (per CRA Art. 13(8)) — and where applicable, OSS-steward cybersecurity policy fostering secure development and effective vulnerability handling (per Art. 24(1) 4-element (i)-(iv)) with verifiable documentation + cooperation with market surveillance authorities (per Art. 24(2)), with the presumption-of-conformity machinery via harmonised standards / common specifications / CSA certification (per Art. 27(1)/(2)/(5)/(8)/(9)).

  fit_criterion: |
    Manufacturer policies/processes/procedures are documented under Art. 13(8) sentence 6 `appropriate policies including a CVD policy` + Annex I Part II §5 CVD policy + Art. 13(8) sentence 2 support-period factors + Art. 13(23) cessation-of-operations communication procedure (SR-CRA-075) with GV.PO-01 policy-instrument layer (CVD policy + support-period-determination methodology established, communicated to security researchers, users, and other stakeholders via publication and promotion, and enforced through the documented policy framework) + GV.PO-02 implementation-process layer (policies operationalised through documented processes — CVD coordination workflow + support-period calculation procedure + policy-update mechanism — with implementation evidence recorded in technical documentation under Annex VII §2(b)); for OSS-steward entities, an Art. 24(1) cybersecurity policy is put in place and documented in a verifiable manner fostering the secure development of products with digital elements and effective vulnerability handling (SR-CRA-072) with GV.PO-01 policy-instrument layer (cybersecurity policy established, communicated across the steward's maintainer base + contributors + downstream dependents, and enforced through the documented 4-element (i)-(iv) `documenting, addressing, remediating vulnerabilities; sharing of information concerning discovered vulnerabilities within the open-source community`) + GV.SC-01 supply-chain-risk-management dimension (policy positioned as the entry point of the steward's SCRM programme, with upstream-coverage of OSS contributors and downstream-coverage of dependents integrated into the programme); the OSS-steward verifiable-documentation character of the policy supplies the auditability anchor under Art. 24(2) MSA cooperation (SR-CRA-073) — upon reasoned request the steward provides documentation of the cybersecurity policy and the relevant security aspects of the products they steward, with GV.SC-04 third-party-assessment dimension (MSA treated as a regulated assessor; cooperation duty operationalises the third-party-assessment-on-request function with documentation of policy + security aspects supplying the assessment record) + GV.OC-03 legal-and-regulatory-requirements-managed dimension (steward's regulatory requirements managed, with the request-triggered nature of the duty preserved — no standing reporting or audit); the presumption-of-conformity machinery is tracked at the conformity-assessment evidence layer via Art. 27(1) harmonised standards (SR-CRA-077) + Art. 27(2)/(5) common specifications via implementing acts + Art. 27(8) CSA certification at the assurance level specified by Art. 8(1) `substantial` or Art. 32(2)/(3) `high`, with GV.OC-03 standing duty to track the Official Journal list of harmonised standards and know which Annex I requirements each cited standard covers + GV.PO-02 common-specification fallback as documented process (the SDLC executes when no harmonised standard exists, and records the alternative solution adopted); the presumption is rebuttable — the OJ literal `presumed` signals a rebuttable presumption rather than a binding acquittal per general EU product-safety case law — and the coverage qualifier `to the extent that the standards cover those requirements` means that an Annex I requirement not addressed by the cited standard is not swept into the presumption and must be demonstrated by the manufacturer (partial-standard coverage shifts the burden back onto the manufacturer for un-addressed elements); for a controller-manufacturer (vertically-integrated entity that processes personal data AND ships CRA-regulated products), the CRA manufacturer-side policies and the GDPR controller-side data-protection policies coexist on different artefacts (manufacturer-side policies for product cybersecurity + controller-side data-protection policies for personal-data processing) on the GDPR↔CRA SAME — COMPLEMENTARY pair (different artefacts, different actors — no OJ-level consolidation rule, per CRDA-deep §9.1 GDPR↔CRA verified relationship); for a DORA entity that procures CRA-conformant products, the CRA presumption-of-conformity machinery discharges parts of the DORA supplier-assessment evidence obligation (cross to 6.1) but does not substitute for the DORA framework obligation on the CRA↔DORA SAME — COMPLEMENTARY pair (Conditional Y when DORA entity procures CRA-conformant products, per CRDA-deep §9.1 CRA↔DORA verified relationship); for AI-enabled products that are both CRA-regulated and AI-Act high-risk, the CRA Annex I conformity stack coexists with the AI Act QMS stack on the CRA↔AI_Act SAME — COMPLEMENTARY pair (CRA on the product cybersecurity requirements + AI Act on the AI-system quality-management system; no OJ-level consolidation rule at 9.1 — unlike 9.2 where CRA Art. 13(4) sentence 2 permits AI Act Art. 9 risk management to substitute for CRA risk assessment, that rule covers the *risk-assessment object* not the *policy object*, per CRDA-deep §9.1 CRA↔AI_Act verified relationship); verified by manufacturer policies/processes/procedures documentation review + Annex VII technical documentation (cross to 9.4) + Art. 24(2) OSS-steward cooperation-on-request records + Annex II §8(a)-(f) instruction-set review (when product-side applies, cross to 8.1 partial) + Annex I Part I + Part II conformity-assessment records + Official Journal harmonised-standards list tracking + common-specification fallback procedure documentation + CSA certification records at the assurance level specified + MSA / ENISA inspection-ready evidence pack on request.

  priority: MUST
  verification_method: TEST
  dependencies: [9.1.1 (when controller-manufacturer — manufacturer-side CRA policies coexist with controller-side GDPR data-protection policies on different artefacts; policies stacked, not collapsed), 9.1.2 (when entity procures CRA-conformant — CRA presumption substitutes for parts of NIS 2 supplier-assessment evidence but not NIS 2 entity-level policy architecture), 9.1.4 (when DORA entity procures CRA-conformant — CRA presumption discharges parts of DORA supplier-assessment evidence but not DORA framework obligation), 9.1.5 (when product is both CRA-regulated and AI-Act high-risk — CRA Annex I conformity + AI Act QMS stack; no OJ-level consolidation rule at 9.1)]

  nist_csf: [GV.PO-01, GV.PO-02, GV.SC-01, GV.SC-04, GV.OC-03]

  applicable_if:
    regs: [CRA]
    scope_overlap: Conditional   # CRA↔GDPR = Conditional (Y when controller is also manufacturer or OSS steward — different artefacts, different actors); CRA↔NIS2 = Conditional (Y when entity procures CRA-conformant products); CRA↔DORA = Conditional (Y when DORA entity procures CRA-conformant products); CRA↔AI_Act = Y (same product is both CRA-regulated and AI-Act high-risk; CRA Annex I conformity + AI Act QMS stack — no OJ-level consolidation rule at 9.1); HL engages on overlap

  satisfaction:
    controls: []
    nodes: []

  considerations: |
    The CRA information-security-policy obligation at 9.1 is the **manufacturer-side / OSS-steward-side policies** with **presumption-of-conformity machinery** via harmonised standards / common specifications / CSA certification. The manufacturer policies cluster (Art. 13(8) + Annex I Part II §5 CVD policy + Annex I Part II §7 secure-update distribution + Art. 13(8) sentence 2 support-period factors + Art. 13(23) cessation-of-operations) is the operational-instrument layer; the OSS-steward policy under Art. 24(1) has 4-element partial content codification (i)-(iv) per the OJ — voluntary-reporting enablement (i), nature-aware proportionality (ii), documenting/addressing/remediating vulnerabilities (iii), sharing of information within the open-source community (iv) — and the `documented in a verifiable manner` (VAG-S3) is read at the R1 literal — `verifiable manner` = the steward can demonstrate the policy on request, with Art. 24(2) MSA cooperation providing external verification on demand (per SR-CRA-072 ambiguity_notes). The Art. 24(2) cooperation duty is request-triggered — no standing reporting or audit obligation (per SR-CRA-073 regulatory_rationale). The Art. 27 presumption-of-conformity machinery is rebuttable — a market-surveillance authority remains free to verify Annex I compliance on substance — and partial-standard coverage shifts the burden back onto the manufacturer for un-addressed elements (per SR-CRA-077 ambiguity_notes). The recipient is the **MSA (market surveillance authority)** on Art. 24(1) OSS-steward policy + Art. 13(8) manufacturer policies/processes/procedures + Art. 27(1)/(2)/(5)/(8)/(9) conformity-assessment records + Annex VII technical documentation (cross to 9.4). For a controller-manufacturer (vertically-integrated entity that processes personal data AND ships CRA-regulated products), the CRA manufacturer-side policies and the GDPR controller-side data-protection policies coexist on different artefacts on the GDPR↔CRA SAME — COMPLEMENTARY pair (different artefacts, different actors — no OJ-level consolidation rule). For an NSIE that procures CRA-conformant products, the CRA presumption-of-conformity machinery substitutes for parts of the NIS 2 supplier-assessment evidence obligation (cross to 6.1) but does not substitute for the NIS 2 entity-level policy architecture on the NIS2↔CRA SAME — COMPLEMENTARY pair. For a DORA entity that procures CRA-conformant products, the CRA presumption-of-conformity machinery discharges parts of the DORA supplier-assessment evidence obligation (cross to 6.1) but does not substitute for the DORA framework obligation on the CRA↔DORA SAME — COMPLEMENTARY pair. For AI-enabled products that are both CRA-regulated and AI-Act high-risk, the CRA Annex I conformity stack coexists with the AI Act QMS stack on the CRA↔AI_Act SAME — COMPLEMENTARY pair (no OJ-level consolidation rule at 9.1 — unlike 9.2 where CRA Art. 13(4) sentence 2 permits AI Act Art. 9 risk management to substitute for CRA risk assessment, that rule covers the *risk-assessment object* not the *policy object*). (Copied from the HSO Considerations block of `### 9.1.3`; preserved here for self-contained traceability.)
```

#### 9.1.4 — Financial-entity ICT risk-management framework with management-body define/approve/oversee/be-responsible and three-lines-of-defence

```yaml
- req_id: 9.1.4
  volere_section: 15
  volere_subtype: integrity

  title: "Financial entity operates a sound, comprehensive, well-documented ICT risk-management framework with three-lines-of-defence and identified, classified, documented ICT supported business functions and assets"

  derives_from:
    hso_sub_so: SO-9.1.DORA
    per_reg_so: SO-DORA-016 + SO-DORA-017
    source_sr: SR-DORA-026 + SR-DORA-027
    hso_file: ../../HierarchicalSecurityObjectives/D-09_Governance-Documentation/9.1.md
    objective_ref_section: "### 9.1.4"

  description: |
    The product shall establish and maintain a sound, comprehensive, and well-documented ICT risk-management framework comprising strategies, policies, procedures, ICT protocols, and tools, with the management body defining, approving, overseeing, and being responsible for its implementation (per DORA Art. 5(2) four-verb coordination + Art. 6(1) framework + Art. 6(2) 5-artefact closed list), with responsibility for managing and overseeing ICT risk assigned to a control function with appropriate level of independence according to the three lines of defence model (per Art. 6(4)), and with ICT supported business functions, information assets, and ICT assets identified, classified, and adequately documented (per Art. 7(1)).

  fit_criterion: |
    A sound, comprehensive, and well-documented ICT risk-management framework is established under Art. 6(1) `which enables them to address ICT risk quickly, efficiently and comprehensively` (SR-DORA-027) with GV.PO-01 + GV.PO-02 + GV.RM-04 (the framework documents the entity's approach to ICT risk, supports the operational-procedure layer with documented processes and procedures, and couples policy formulation to strategic-direction and update-coupling); the framework comprises the Art. 6(2) 5-artefact closed list `strategies, policies, procedures, ICT protocols and tools`; the management body discharges the Art. 5(2) four-verb coordination (SR-DORA-026) `define, approve, oversee and be responsible for the implementation of all arrangements related to the ICT risk management framework` with GV.RR-01 leadership accountability + GV.RR-02 approval as documented governance act (the approval minute records the management body's reading of the framework, acceptance of residual risk, and resource commitment, creating executive sponsorship) + GV.OC-03 standing duty to track the full set of legal, regulatory, and contractual obligations that DORA adds on top of CRA, NIS 2, GDPR, and AI Act; the `define, approve, oversee and be responsible for` (S2 VAG + POLY + COORD per SR-DORA-026 ambiguity_notes) four-verb coordination carries the ambiguity on whether the four verbs are four distinct obligations or a composite — chosen reading: four distinct obligations (the OJ coordinates four verbs with commas and the conjunction `and`); the responsibility for managing and overseeing ICT risk is assigned to a control function with appropriate level of independence according to the three lines of defence model (or an internal risk management and control model) per Art. 6(4) (SR-DORA-027) with GV.RR-02 + GV.OC-03 (the three-lines-of-defence model is operationalised as a documented control-function independence architecture, with the operational-management function as first line, the risk-management and compliance function as second line, and the internal-audit function as third line, each with documented independence and reporting lines); ICT supported business functions, roles and responsibilities, the information assets and ICT assets supporting those functions, and their roles and dependencies in relation to ICT risk are identified, classified and adequately documented per Art. 7(1) (SR-DORA-027 cross) with GV.OC-04 critical-objectives-and-capabilities understanding (the entity understands and communicates the ICT-supported business functions, the assets supporting those functions, and their roles and dependencies in relation to ICT risk) + GV.RM-05 upward-channel substrate (the CISO and relevant functions have documented escalation paths to the management body, including exception and incident reporting); the framework is documented under Art. 5(2) management-body oversight + EBA Guidelines on Internal Governance supervisory baseline + EBA Guidelines on ICT and security risk management; for smaller entities under Art. 4 proportionality, the framework is proportionate to the entity's risk exposure, size, and complexity; for a DORA financial entity that is also an NSIE, the DORA Art. 6(1) ICT risk-management framework discharges both DORA's dedicated framework obligation and NIS 2's Art. 21(2)(a)+(f) policy artefacts + Art. 20(1) governance act on the NIS 2 ↔ DORA SAME — EQUAL pair (DORA-dominant stack for financial entities, per CRDA-deep §9.1 NIS2↔DORA verified relationship); for a DORA financial entity that is also a controller, the DORA Art. 6(1) ICT risk-management framework is the carrier and the GDPR Art. 24(1) data-protection policies are a module within the framework on the GDPR↔DORA SAME — COMPLEMENTARY pair (DORA-dominant for financial entities, per CRDA-deep §9.1 GDPR↔DORA verified relationship); for a DORA entity that procures CRA-conformant products, the CRA presumption-of-conformity machinery discharges parts of the DORA supplier-assessment evidence obligation (cross to 6.1) but does not substitute for the DORA framework obligation on the CRA↔DORA SAME — COMPLEMENTARY pair (Conditional Y when DORA entity procures CRA-conformant products, per CRDA-deep §9.1 CRA↔DORA verified relationship); for a DORA financial entity that deploys high-risk AI, the DORA ICT risk-management framework + AI Act QMS + Art. 26(1) deployer measures stack on the DORA↔AI_Act SAME — COMPLEMENTARY pair (three-lines-of-defence + QMS compatible governance architectures; DORA-led stack with AI Act QMS as sub-component, per CRDA-deep §9.1 DORA↔AI_Act verified relationship); verified by Art. 6(1) framework documentation review + Art. 5(2) management-body oversight records + Art. 6(2) 5-artefact closed-list records + Art. 6(4) three-lines-of-defence model documentation + Art. 7(1) ICT asset register + asset classification records + EBA Guidelines on ICT and security risk management supervisory-baseline documentation + NCA (national financial supervisory authority) review on request.

  priority: MUST
  verification_method: TEST
  dependencies: [8.3.2 (Art. 5(4) management-body training as literacy prerequisite for Art. 5(2) define/approve/oversee/be-responsible), 9.1.2 (when DORA-NSIE — DORA Art. 6(1) ICT risk-management framework discharges both DORA's dedicated framework obligation and NIS 2's Art. 21(2)(a)+(f) policy artefacts + Art. 20(1) governance act; EQUAL classification, DORA-dominant stack for financial entities), 9.1.1 (when DORA-controller — DORA framework as carrier + GDPR data-protection policies as module; DORA-dominant stack), 9.1.3 (when DORA entity procures CRA-conformant — CRA presumption discharges parts of DORA supplier-assessment evidence but not DORA framework obligation), 9.1.5 (when DORA FE deploys high-risk AI — DORA ICT risk-management framework + AI Act QMS + Art. 26(1) deployer measures stack; three-lines-of-defence + QMS compatible governance architectures; DORA-led stack with AI Act QMS as sub-component)]

  nist_csf: [GV.PO-01, GV.PO-02, GV.RM-04, GV.RR-01, GV.RR-02, GV.OC-03, GV.OC-04, GV.RM-05]

  applicable_if:
    regs: [DORA]
    scope_overlap: Conditional   # DORA↔NIS2 = Y (DORA financial entity = NSIE; DORA-dominant stack for financial entities — EQUAL on policy + governance dimension); DORA↔GDPR = Y (DORA financial entity = controller; DORA framework as carrier + GDPR data-protection policies as module; DORA-dominant for financial entities); DORA↔CRA = Conditional (Y when DORA entity procures CRA-conformant products); DORA↔AI_Act = Y (DORA FE deploys high-risk AI; DORA ICT risk-management framework + AI Act QMS + Art. 26(1) deployer measures stack; three-lines-of-defence + QMS compatible governance architectures; DORA-led stack with AI Act QMS as sub-component); HL engages on overlap

  satisfaction:
    controls: []
    nodes: []

  considerations: |
    The DORA information-security-policy obligation at 9.1 is the **financial-entity ICT risk-management framework** with the **management-body define/approve/oversee/be-responsible architecture** (Art. 5(2) four-verb coordination) + **three-lines-of-defence** (Art. 6(4)) + **ICT asset identification / classification / documentation** (Art. 7(1)) as governance anchor. Art. 6(1) `sound, comprehensive and well-documented ICT risk management framework` carries S2 VAG + POLY + COORD on `sound, comprehensive and well-documented` (three vague adjectives — operational thresholds supplied by EBA Guidelines on ICT and security risk management) and on `quickly, efficiently and comprehensively` (S3 VAG + POLY + COORD per SR-DORA-027 ambiguity_notes); Art. 6(2) 5-artefact closed list `strategies, policies, procedures, ICT protocols and tools` — full content codification (vs GDPR's entity-discretionary content under Art. 24(2) or NIS 2's entity-discretionary content under Recital 56 proportionality); Art. 5(2) four-verb coordination `define, approve, oversee and be responsible for` carries S2 VAG + POLY + COORD ambiguity on whether the four verbs are four distinct obligations, one composite obligation, or other combinations — chosen reading: four distinct obligations (per SR-DORA-026 ambiguity_notes, the OJ coordinates four verbs with commas and the conjunction `and`); Art. 6(4) `three lines of defence model` carries S2 VAG + POLY + COORD on `appropriate level of independence` (chosen reading: operational-management as first line, risk-management and compliance as second line, internal-audit as third line); Art. 7(1) `identify, classify and adequately document` 3-verb + long AND chain of asset classes (ICT supported business functions + roles and responsibilities + information assets + ICT assets + their roles and dependencies in relation to ICT risk). The `actively keep up to date` training obligation for the management body (Art. 5(4), cross-ref 8.3.2) is the literacy underpinning of Art. 5(2) management-body responsibility and the personal-capacity threshold for Art. 5(2) to operate meaningfully. For smaller entities under Art. 4 proportionality, the framework is proportionate to the entity's risk exposure. The recipient is the **competent authority (NCA — national financial supervisory authority) + management body** on Art. 6(1) ICT risk-management framework + Art. 5(2) management-body oversight + EBA Guidelines on ICT and security risk management supervisory baseline. For a DORA financial entity that is also an NSIE, the DORA Art. 6(1) ICT risk-management framework discharges both DORA's dedicated framework obligation and NIS 2's Art. 21(2)(a)+(f) policy artefacts + Art. 20(1) governance act on the NIS 2 ↔ DORA SAME — EQUAL pair (DORA-dominant stack for financial entities). For a DORA financial entity that is also a controller, the DORA Art. 6(1) ICT risk-management framework is the carrier and the GDPR Art. 24(1) data-protection policies are a module within the framework on the GDPR↔DORA SAME — COMPLEMENTARY pair. For a DORA entity that procures CRA-conformant products, the CRA presumption-of-conformity machinery discharges parts of the DORA supplier-assessment evidence obligation (cross to 6.1) but does not substitute for the DORA framework obligation on the CRA↔DORA SAME — COMPLEMENTARY pair. For a DORA financial entity that deploys high-risk AI, the DORA ICT risk-management framework + AI Act QMS + Art. 26(1) deployer measures stack on the DORA↔AI_Act SAME — COMPLEMENTARY pair (three-lines-of-defence + QMS compatible governance architectures). (Copied from the HSO Considerations block of `### 9.1.4`; preserved here for self-contained traceability.)
```

#### 9.1.5 — Provider-side QMS documented in a systematic and orderly manner with 13-element closed list and transparency-to-deployers instructions-for-use

```yaml
- req_id: 9.1.5
  volere_section: 15
  volere_subtype: integrity

  title: "Provider of high-risk AI system operates a documented quality management system with at least 13 enumerated aspects and instructions for use enabling deployer-interpretable transparency"

  derives_from:
    hso_sub_so: SO-9.1.AI_Act
    per_reg_so: SO-AIACT-007 + SO-AIACT-010
    source_sr: SR-AIACT-010 + SR-AIACT-017
    hso_file: ../../HierarchicalSecurityObjectives/D-09_Governance-Documentation/9.1.md
    objective_ref_section: "### 9.1.5"

  description: |
    The product shall operate a documented quality management system (QMS) ensuring compliance with the AI Act, documented in a systematic and orderly manner in the form of written policies, procedures, and instructions covering at least the 13 enumerated aspects of Art. 17(1) closed list (a)-(m), with high-risk AI systems designed and developed to ensure sufficiently transparent operation enabling deployers to interpret a system's output and use it appropriately, accompanied by concise, complete, correct and clear instructions for use (per AI Act Art. 17(1) + Art. 13(1) + Art. 13(3)).

  fit_criterion: |
    A QMS is documented under Art. 17(1) (SR-AIACT-017) in a systematic and orderly manner in the form of written policies, procedures, and instructions (POLY on `quality management system` — R1 ISO 9001 / R2 ISO/IEC 23053 / R3 internal bespoke; chosen reading: R3 literal since the OJ does not name any specific standard — Recital 67 characterises the QMS as inspired by established quality standards but does not mandate a specific standard); the QMS covers at least the 13 enumerated aspects (a)-(m) of the Art. 17(1) closed list with GV.PO-01 policy arm (the top-level written cybersecurity policy that operationalises the 13-element list including the policies, procedures, and instructions anchor) + GV.PO-02 process-and-procedure arm (the policies accompanied by documented processes and procedures, also documented, communicated, and enforced — the systematic-and-orderly requirement of Art. 17(1)) + GV.RM-01 risk-management-objectives binding (the QMS bound to risk-management objectives agreed by organisational stakeholders, satisfying sub-point (g)'s cross-reference to Art. 9 risk-management system) + GV.RM-04 strategic-direction layer (risk appetite, response posture, escalation criteria that connect policy formulation and risk-objective setting to operational risk-treatment decisions) + GV.OC-03 legal-and-regulatory obligations register (the QMS understands and manages the full set of legal, regulatory, and contractual obligations that the AI Act adds on top of CRA, NIS 2, GDPR, and DORA); high-risk AI systems are designed and developed to ensure sufficiently transparent operation enabling deployers to interpret a system's output and use it appropriately under Art. 13(1) (SR-AIACT-010) with GV.PO-01 cybersecurity-policy substrate (transparency must be policy-bound and enforced to be supervisory-auditable) + GV.OC-03 legal-and-regulatory understanding substrate (distinguishing the high-risk-specific Art. 13(1) transparency duty from the Art. 50 user-facing and Art. 70 deployer-information duties, ensuring the cumulative AND of explainability, disclosure, and interpretability senses is operationalised against an understood regulatory baseline) + GV.OC-04 stakeholder-communication dimension (deployers are the principal stakeholders for AI-system transparency, operationalising the `interpret a system's output AND use it appropriately` opening through understood capabilities and limitations); the chosen reading for `sufficiently transparent` + `appropriate type and degree of transparency` is cumulative (explainability + disclosure + interpretability) because the `interpret a system's output AND use it appropriately` opening implicates each sense (per SR-AIACT-010 ambiguity_notes — central S3 ambiguity on `sufficiently transparent` admits three AI-governance senses: explainability / disclosure / interpretability, each implying different technical implementations — explainable-AI tooling / system documentation / model-architecture disclosure respectively); Art. 13(3) instructions-for-use are `concise, complete, correct and clear` (4-way AND) + `relevant, accessible and comprehensible` (3-way AND) + `characteristics, capabilities and limitations of performance` (POLY triple) — the high-risk-system transparency instructions-for-use accompanying the system; the QMS is documented under Art. 17(1) systematic-and-orderly QMS + Art. 9 risk-management cross-reference + Art. 43/47 conformity-assessment accountability chain; the QMS becomes a sub-policy of the integrated policy architecture on the GDPR↔AI_Act SAME — COMPLEMENTARY pair (AI Act Recital 78 explicit GDPR preservation — AI Act QMS as sub-policy of GDPR data-protection policies for providers/deployers that are controllers processing personal data via high-risk AI, per CRDA-deep §9.1 GDPR↔AI_Act verified relationship); for an NSIE that deploys high-risk AI, the AI Act QMS becomes a sub-policy of the entity-level NIS 2 governance framework on the NIS2↔AI_Act SAME — COMPLEMENTARY pair (the AI Act layer adds the AI-specific 13-element QMS to the NIS 2 risk-analysis policy, per CRDA-deep §9.1 NIS2↔AI_Act verified relationship); for AI-enabled products that are both CRA-regulated and AI-Act high-risk, the CRA Annex I conformity stack coexists with the AI Act QMS stack on the CRA↔AI_Act SAME — COMPLEMENTARY pair (CRA on the product cybersecurity requirements + AI Act on the AI-system quality-management system — no OJ-level consolidation rule at 9.1, unlike 9.2 where CRA Art. 13(4) sentence 2 permits AI Act Art. 9 risk management to substitute for CRA risk assessment, that rule covers the *risk-assessment object* not the *policy object*, per CRDA-deep §9.1 CRA↔AI_Act verified relationship); for a DORA financial entity that deploys high-risk AI, the DORA ICT risk-management framework + AI Act QMS + Art. 26(1) deployer measures stack on the DORA↔AI_Act SAME — COMPLEMENTARY pair (three-lines-of-defence + QMS compatible governance architectures; DORA-led stack with AI Act QMS as sub-component, per CRDA-deep §9.1 DORA↔AI_Act verified relationship); verified by Art. 17(1) QMS documentation review + Art. 13(1) sufficiently-transparent design-and-development evidence + Art. 13(3) `concise, complete, correct and clear` instructions-for-use documentation review + Art. 17(1)(g) Art. 9 risk-management-system cross-reference documentation + Art. 43/47 conformity-assessment records + Art. 50 user-facing transparency records (when applicable) + MSA / AI Office / provider / deployer inspection-ready evidence pack on request.

  priority: MUST
  verification_method: TEST
  dependencies: [9.1.1 (when provider/deployer = controller — AI Act QMS as sub-policy of GDPR data-protection policies; AI Act Recital 78 explicit GDPR preservation), 9.1.2 (when NSIE deploys high-risk AI — AI Act QMS as sub-policy of NIS 2 entity-level governance framework), 9.1.3 (when product is both CRA-regulated and AI-Act high-risk — CRA Annex I conformity + AI Act QMS stack; no OJ-level consolidation rule at 9.1), 9.1.4 (when DORA FE deploys high-risk AI — DORA ICT risk-management framework + AI Act QMS + Art. 26(1) deployer measures stack; three-lines-of-defence + QMS compatible governance architectures; DORA-led stack with AI Act QMS as sub-component)]

  nist_csf: [GV.PO-01, GV.PO-02, GV.RM-01, GV.RM-04, GV.OC-03, GV.OC-04]

  applicable_if:
    regs: [AI_Act]
    scope_overlap: Conditional   # AI_Act↔GDPR = Y (provider/deployer = controller; AI Act QMS as sub-policy of GDPR data-protection policies; AI Act Recital 78 explicit GDPR preservation); AI_Act↔NIS2 = Y (NSIE deploys high-risk AI; AI Act QMS as sub-policy of NIS 2 entity-level governance framework); AI_Act↔CRA = Y (same product is both CRA-regulated and AI-Act high-risk; CRA Annex I conformity + AI Act QMS stack — no OJ-level consolidation rule at 9.1); AI_Act↔DORA = Y (DORA FE deploys high-risk AI; DORA ICT risk-management framework + AI Act QMS + Art. 26(1) deployer measures stack; three-lines-of-defence + QMS compatible governance architectures); HL engages on overlap

  satisfaction:
    controls: []
    nodes: []

  considerations: |
    The AI Act information-security-policy obligation at 9.1 is the **provider-side QMS** documented in a systematic and orderly manner in the form of written policies, procedures, and instructions (Art. 17(1)) with the **transparency-to-deployers instructions-for-use** (Art. 13(1)+(3)) as policy-content dimension. Art. 17(1) carries S2 features — `systematic and orderly manner` is VAG-2 (Berry §5.1 — the OJ does not anchor the sense); `quality management system` is POLY-2 with R1 ISO 9001 / R2 ISO/IEC 23053 / R3 internal bespoke — chosen reading: R3 literal (OJ does not name any specific standard); the 3-way AND of `written policies, procedures AND instructions` and the 13-element closed list (a)-(m) are both COORD-2 (per SR-AIACT-017 ambiguity_notes). The 13 enumerated aspects include (a) compliance strategy; (b) design, development, and verification techniques; (c) design, design control, design verification of the high-risk AI system; (d) post-market monitoring; (e) data quality; (f) ... (m) — full content codification (vs GDPR's entity-discretionary content under Art. 24(2) or NIS 2's entity-discretionary content under Recital 56 proportionality). Art. 13(1) `sufficiently transparent` + `appropriate type and degree of transparency` is the central S3 ambiguity (per SR-AIACT-010 ambiguity_notes) — admits three AI-governance senses (explainability / disclosure / interpretability) with different technical implementations — chosen reading: cumulative covering all three senses because the `interpret a system's output AND use it appropriately` opening implicates each. Art. 13(3) `concise, complete, correct and clear` (4-way AND) + `relevant, accessible and comprehensible` (3-way AND) + `characteristics, capabilities and limitations of performance` (POLY triple) — twin 4-way and 3-way adjective coordinations. Art. 17(1)(g) cross-refers to Art. 9 risk-management system, so the QMS is the governance substrate for the substantive Title III obligations. The recipient is the **provider, deployer, MSA, AI Office** on Art. 17(1) QMS documented policies/procedures/instructions + Art. 13(1)+(3) transparency instructions-for-use + Art. 26(1) deployer measures aligned with instructions. For a provider/deployer that is a controller, the AI Act QMS becomes a sub-policy of the GDPR data-protection policies on the GDPR↔AI_Act SAME — COMPLEMENTARY pair (AI Act Recital 78 explicit GDPR preservation). For an NSIE that deploys high-risk AI, the AI Act QMS becomes a sub-policy of the entity-level NIS 2 governance framework on the NIS2↔AI_Act SAME — COMPLEMENTARY pair (the AI Act layer adds the AI-specific 13-element QMS to the NIS 2 risk-analysis policy). For AI-enabled products that are both CRA-regulated and AI-Act high-risk, the CRA Annex I conformity stack coexists with the AI Act QMS stack on the CRA↔AI_Act SAME — COMPLEMENTARY pair (CRA on the product cybersecurity requirements + AI Act on the AI-system quality-management system — no OJ-level consolidation rule at 9.1, unlike 9.2 where CRA Art. 13(4) sentence 2 permits AI Act Art. 9 risk management to substitute for CRA risk assessment). For a DORA financial entity that deploys high-risk AI, the DORA ICT risk-management framework + AI Act QMS + Art. 26(1) deployer measures stack on the DORA↔AI_Act SAME — COMPLEMENTARY pair (three-lines-of-defence + QMS compatible governance architectures). (Copied from the HSO Considerations block of `### 9.1.5`; preserved here for self-contained traceability.)
```

> **Deep analysis:** [D-09.1 Information Security Policies](../../DeepAnalysis/D-09_Governance-Documentation/D-09.1.md)

#### D-09.1 Information Security Policies

<!-- participants: GDPR, NIS2, CRA, DORA, AI_Act -->

##### Participants

| Regulation | SO ID | 1-line summary | Scope/angle |
|---|---|---|---|
| GDPR | SO-GDPR-026 + SO-GDPR-027 + SO-GDPR-037 | Appropriate data-protection policies reviewed and updated; controller responsibility; DPO designation + independence (Art. 24, Art. 5(2), Art. 37/38/39). | Process-oriented data-protection policies; DPO as governance anchor. |
| NIS2 | SO-NIS2-021 + SO-NIS2-022 + SO-NIS2-023 | Management body approves/oversees/liable; policies on risk analysis + IS security; policies to assess effectiveness (Art. 20(1), Art. 21(2)(a)+(f)). | Governance-first with policy artefact; management-body accountability architecture. |
| CRA | SO-CRA-049 + SO-CRA-050 + SO-CRA-051 + SO-CRA-052 | OSS steward cybersecurity policy; manufacturer policies/processes/procedures; presumption of conformity via harmonised standards / common specs / CSA certification (Art. 13(8), Art. 24, Art. 27). | Product-level policy framework + standards-presumption machinery. |
| DORA | SO-DORA-016 + SO-DORA-017 | Management body defines/approves/oversees/implements ICT risk management framework; three-lines-of-defence + asset identification (Art. 5(2), Art. 6(1)/(4), Art. 7(1)). | Financial-entity ICT risk management framework; three-lines-of-defence as governance anchor. |
| AI_Act | SO-AIACT-007 + SO-AIACT-010 + SO-AIACT-011 | Transparency to deployers; QMS with 13 enumerated aspects; deployer measures aligned with instructions (Art. 13, Art. 17, Art. 26). | AI-system-specific policies via QMS; transparency-as-policy. |

##### Pairwise matrix

<!-- pair: GDPR,NIS2 -->
| GDPR ↔ NIS2 | **complementary** | Tension/difference |
|---|---|---|
| **GDPR** (SR-GDPR-043, Art. 24(1)+(2); SR-GDPR-044, Art. 5(2); SR-GDPR-062/063/064, Art. 37-39) | Appropriate technical and organisational measures; demonstrable compliance; DPO designation and independence. | |
|   → full rationale: `../../../Regulation/GDPR/02_SecurityRules_NIST.md` SR-GDPR-043 / SR-GDPR-044 / SR-GDPR-062-064 |
| **NIS2** (SR-NIS2-032, Art. 20(1) approve; SR-NIS2-033, Art. 20(1) oversee; SR-NIS2-034, Art. 20(1) liability; SR-NIS2-035, Art. 21(2)(a) risk-analysis policy; SR-NIS2-036, Art. 21(2)(a) IS-security policy; SR-NIS2-037, Art. 21(2)(f) effectiveness-assessment policy) | Management body approve/oversee/liable; risk-analysis policy + IS-security policy + effectiveness-assessment policy. | |
|   → full rationale: `../../../Regulation/NIS2/02_SecurityRules_NIST.md` SR-NIS2-032 / SR-NIS2-033 / SR-NIS2-034 / SR-NIS2-035 / SR-NIS2-036 / SR-NIS2-037 |

**Why complementary**: GDPR mandates the policy artefact (data-protection policies); NIS 2 mandates the governance act (management body approves/oversees/liable) AND the policy artefact. NIS 2's governance architecture (approve/oversee/liable) extends to the GDPR policies for entities that are both controllers and NSIEs.


<!-- /pair -->

<!-- pair: GDPR,CRA -->
| GDPR ↔ CRA | **complementary** | Tension/difference |
|---|---|---|
| **GDPR** (SR-GDPR-043, Art. 24(1)+(2)) | Appropriate technical and organisational measures; data-protection policies. | |
|   → full rationale: `../../../Regulation/GDPR/02_SecurityRules_NIST.md` SR-GDPR-043 |
| **CRA** (SR-CRA-072, Art. 24(1) OSS steward policy; SR-CRA-073, Art. 24(2) OSS steward cooperation; SR-CRA-075, Art. 13(8) manufacturer policies cluster; SR-CRA-077, Art. 27(1)/(2)/(5) presumption via harmonised standards / common specs; SR-CRA-078, Art. 27(8)/(9) CSA-certification presumption) | OSS steward cybersecurity policy; manufacturer policies/processes/procedures; presumption of conformity. | |
|   → full rationale: `../../../Regulation/CRA/02_SecurityRules_NIST.md` SR-CRA-072 / SR-CRA-073 / SR-CRA-075 / SR-CRA-077 / SR-CRA-078 |

**Why complementary**: GDPR addresses controller policies for personal data processing; CRA addresses manufacturer policies for product cybersecurity. The two policies are different artefacts and can coexist without overlap.


<!-- /pair -->

<!-- pair: GDPR,DORA -->
| GDPR ↔ DORA | **complementary** | Tension/difference |
|---|---|---|
| **GDPR** (SR-GDPR-043, Art. 24(1)+(2)) | Appropriate data-protection policies. | |
|   → full rationale: `../../../Regulation/GDPR/02_SecurityRules_NIST.md` SR-GDPR-043 |
| **DORA** (SR-DORA-026, Art. 5(2) management body defines/approves/oversees/implements; SR-DORA-027, Art. 6(1) sound/comprehensive/well-documented ICT risk management framework; SR-DORA-028, Art. 6(4) three-lines-of-defence + asset identification) | ICT risk management framework with three-lines-of-defence model and asset identification/classification. | |
|   → full rationale: `../../../Regulation/DORA/02_SecurityRules_NIST.md` SR-DORA-026 / SR-DORA-027 / SR-DORA-028 |

**Why complementary**: DORA's ICT risk management framework is the dominant authority for financial entities; GDPR policies become a module within the framework. DORA Art. 5(2) governance + GDPR Art. 5(2) accountability stack.


<!-- /pair -->

<!-- pair: GDPR,AI_Act -->
| GDPR ↔ AI_Act | **complementary** | Tension/difference |
|---|---|---|
| **GDPR** (SR-GDPR-043, Art. 24(1)+(2)) | Appropriate data-protection policies. | |
|   → full rationale: `../../../Regulation/GDPR/02_SecurityRules_NIST.md` SR-GDPR-043 |
| **AI_Act** (SR-AIACT-010, Art. 13(1)+(3); SR-AIACT-011, Art. 17(1); SR-AIACT-017, Art. 17(1) QMS; SR-AIACT-019, Art. 26(1) deployer measures aligned with instructions) | Transparency to deployers + instructions for use; QMS with 13 enumerated aspects; deployer-side measures. | |
|   → full rationale: `../../../Regulation/AI_Act/02_SecurityRules_NIST.md` SR-AIACT-010 / SR-AIACT-011 / SR-AIACT-017 / SR-AIACT-019 |

**Why complementary**: GDPR data-protection policies stack with AI Act QMS policies; AI Act does not displace GDPR for personal-data processing. AI Act Recital 78 explicitly preserves GDPR.


<!-- /pair -->

<!-- pair: NIS2,CRA -->
| NIS2 ↔ CRA | **complementary** | Tension/difference |
|---|---|---|
| **NIS2** (SR-NIS2-035, Art. 21(2)(a); SR-NIS2-036, Art. 21(2)(a); SR-NIS2-037, Art. 21(2)(f)) | Risk-analysis policy + IS-security policy + effectiveness-assessment policy. | |
|   → full rationale: `../../../Regulation/NIS2/02_SecurityRules_NIST.md` SR-NIS2-035 / SR-NIS2-036 / SR-NIS2-037 |
| **CRA** (SR-CRA-077, Art. 27(1)/(2)/(5); SR-CRA-078, Art. 27(8)/(9)) | Presumption of conformity via harmonised standards / common specs / CSA certification. | |
|   → full rationale: `../../../Regulation/CRA/02_SecurityRules_NIST.md` SR-CRA-077 / SR-CRA-078 |

**Why complementary**: NIS 2 mandates the policy artefact; CRA provides the presumption-of-conformity machinery that the NIS 2 entity can rely on when procuring CRA-conformant products. The CRA presumption substitutes for parts of the NIS 2 supplier-assessment obligation.


<!-- /pair -->

<!-- pair: NIS2,DORA -->
| NIS2 ↔ DORA | **equal** | Tension/difference |
|---|---|---|
| **NIS2** (SR-NIS2-032, Art. 20(1); SR-NIS2-035, Art. 21(2)(a); SR-NIS2-037, Art. 21(2)(f)) | Management body approve/oversee/liable; risk-analysis + IS-security + effectiveness-assessment policies. | |
|   → full rationale: `../../../Regulation/NIS2/02_SecurityRules_NIST.md` SR-NIS2-032 / SR-NIS2-035 / SR-NIS2-037 |
| **DORA** (SR-DORA-026, Art. 5(2); SR-DORA-027, Art. 6(1); SR-DORA-028, Art. 6(4)) | ICT risk management framework with management body ownership and three-lines-of-defence. | |
|   → full rationale: `../../../Regulation/DORA/02_SecurityRules_NIST.md` SR-DORA-026 / SR-DORA-027 / SR-DORA-028 |

**Why equal for financial entities**: Both regulations impose policy + governance architecture. DORA's ICT risk management framework is the dominant authority for financial entities under DORA's direct scope. NIS 2 applies to the financial entity only insofar as it is an NSIE (essential/important entity) outside DORA.


<!-- /pair -->

<!-- pair: NIS2,AI_Act -->
| NIS2 ↔ AI_Act | **complementary** | Tension/difference |
|---|---|---|
| **NIS2** (SR-NIS2-032, Art. 20(1); SR-NIS2-035, Art. 21(2)(a)) | Management body approval + risk-analysis policy. | |
|   → full rationale: `../../../Regulation/NIS2/02_SecurityRules_NIST.md` SR-NIS2-032 / SR-NIS2-035 |
| **AI_Act** (SR-AIACT-017, Art. 17(1) QMS) | QMS with 13 enumerated aspects as the AI-system policy carrier. | |
|   → full rationale: `../../../Regulation/AI_Act/02_SecurityRules_NIST.md` SR-AIACT-017 |

**Why complementary**: NIS 2 governance applies at the entity level; AI Act QMS applies at the AI-system level. For NSIEs deploying high-risk AI, the QMS becomes a sub-policy of the entity-level NIS 2 governance framework.


<!-- /pair -->

<!-- pair: CRA,DORA -->
| CRA ↔ DORA | **complementary** | Tension/difference |
|---|---|---|
| **CRA** (SR-CRA-077, Art. 27(1)/(2)/(5)) | Presumption of conformity via harmonised standards / common specs. | |
|   → full rationale: `../../../Regulation/CRA/02_SecurityRules_NIST.md` SR-CRA-077 |
| **DORA** (SR-DORA-027, Art. 6(1)) | Sound/comprehensive/well-documented ICT risk management framework. | |
|   → full rationale: `../../../Regulation/DORA/02_SecurityRules_NIST.md` SR-DORA-027 |

**Why complementary**: CRA's presumption of conformity via harmonised standards allows DORA entities to rely on CRA-conformant products as inputs to their ICT risk management framework. The two policies operate on different layers (product vs. entity).


<!-- /pair -->

<!-- pair: CRA,AI_Act -->
| CRA ↔ AI_Act | **complementary** | Tension/difference |
|---|---|---|
| **CRA** (SR-CRA-077, Art. 27; SR-CRA-078, Art. 27(8)/(9)) | Presumption of conformity via harmonised standards + CSA certification. | |
|   → full rationale: `../../../Regulation/CRA/02_SecurityRules_NIST.md` SR-CRA-077 / SR-CRA-078 |
| **AI_Act** (SR-AIACT-017, Art. 17(1) QMS) | QMS with 13 enumerated aspects. | |
|   → full rationale: `../../../Regulation/AI_Act/02_SecurityRules_NIST.md` SR-AIACT-017 |

**Why complementary + partial overlap**: For AI-enabled products, CRA Annex I conformity + AI Act QMS stack. CRA Art. 13(4) sentence 2 permits the AI Act Art. 9 risk assessment to substitute for the CRA risk assessment when both apply (see SR-CRA-066 ambiguity_notes). The QMS layer and the CRA presumption-of-conformity machinery are distinct but co-dischargeable.


<!-- /pair -->

<!-- pair: DORA,AI_Act -->
| DORA ↔ AI_Act | **complementary** | Tension/difference |
|---|---|---|
| **DORA** (SR-DORA-026, Art. 5(2); SR-DORA-027, Art. 6(1); SR-DORA-028, Art. 6(4)) | ICT risk management framework + three-lines-of-defence + asset identification. | |
|   → full rationale: `../../../Regulation/DORA/02_SecurityRules_NIST.md` SR-DORA-026 / SR-DORA-027 / SR-DORA-028 |
| **AI_Act** (SR-AIACT-017, Art. 17(1); SR-AIACT-019, Art. 26(1)) | QMS with 13 enumerated aspects + deployer measures aligned with provider instructions. | |
|   → full rationale: `../../../Regulation/AI_Act/02_SecurityRules_NIST.md` SR-AIACT-017 / SR-AIACT-019 |

**Why complementary**: DORA ICT risk management framework + AI Act QMS stack for DORA financial entities deploying high-risk AI. The three-lines-of-defence model and the QMS are compatible governance architectures.


<!-- /pair -->
##### Emergent tensions (5 regulations)
<!-- emergent: GDPR,NIS2,CRA,DORA,AI_Act -->

- **Five-policy-stack with no OJ-level consolidation rule**: D-09.1 is the most heavily-overlapped sub-domain. GDPR (data-protection policy + DPO), NIS 2 (governance + 3 policies), CRA (manufacturer policies + presumption of conformity), DORA (ICT risk management framework + three-lines-of-defence), AI Act (QMS + transparency). All five apply simultaneously to a DORA financial entity that processes personal data and ships AI-enabled CRA-regulated products. There is no OJ-level consolidation rule. Phase 1 entities must build a governance-architecture mapping that nests all five policies without overlap. Recommend Layer 1 cross-cutting analysis.
- **DPO (GDPR) vs. management body (NIS 2 / DORA)**: GDPR's DPO is a designated individual with Art. 39(1) tasks; NIS 2 / DORA's management body is the corporate governance body with collective approve/oversee/liable obligations. The DPO reports to the management body but is not a member of it (Art. 38(6) conflict-of-interests). The interplay between the DPO's advisory role and the management body's liability exposure is unresolved at the OJ level.
- **Presumption of conformity (CRA Art. 27) does not extend cross-regulation**: CRA presumption of conformity via harmonised standards discharges the CRA conformity-assessment obligation only. It does not substitute for the GDPR Art. 24 appropriate-measures obligation, NIS 2 Art. 21(2)(a) policy obligation, or DORA Art. 6(1) ICT risk management framework. Phase 1 entities must discharge each regime's policy obligation separately even when the underlying product is CRA-conformant.

<!-- /emergent -->

##### SR cross-validation
All 5 regulations map D-09.1 to the NIST CSF 2.0 Subcategory **GV.PO-01** (Organizational cybersecurity policy established, communicated, enforced) as the convergent anchor. DORA additionally maps **GV.RR-02** (Roles/responsibilities/accountabilities established) for the three-lines-of-defence architecture; GDPR additionally maps **GV.RM-04** (Strategic direction for risk response) for the data-protection-policy anchor; NIS 2 additionally maps **GV.OV-01** (Risk management strategy outcomes reviewed) for the management-body-oversight dimension.

##### Downstream implication
Phase 1 entities must implement five policy artefacts (or a consolidated policy that nests all five). The Layer 1 governance-architecture mapping is required. The DPO role (GDPR) and the management body (NIS 2 / DORA) must be reconciled in the corporate-governance charter. AI Act QMS becomes a sub-policy. CRA presumption of conformity is the upstream gate for CRA-conformant products but does not discharge cross-regulation obligations.

> **Domain analysis:** [D-09.1 Information Security Policies](../../DomainAnalysis/D-09_Governance-Documentation/D-09.1.md)

#### D-09.1 Information Security Policies

<!-- participants: GDPR, NIS2, CRA, DORA, AI_Act -->

**Participants (from CRDA):** GDPR, NIS2, CRA, DORA, AI_Act

**Critical note (CRDA flag):** D-09.1 is the most heavily-overlapped sub-domain. All 5 regulations overlap with distinct policy artefacts and no OJ-level consolidation rule.

##### Pair: GDPR ↔ NIS2

**GDPR article (verbatim, Art. 24(1) + Art. 5(2) + Art. 37-39):**
> "Taking into account the nature, scope, context and purposes of processing as well as the risks of varying likelihood and severity for the rights and freedoms of natural persons, the controller shall implement appropriate technical and organisational measures, such as … policies … which are designed to implement data-protection principles … in an effective manner." — Art. 24(1) GDPR
> "The controller shall be responsible for, and be able to demonstrate compliance with, paragraph 1 ('accountability')." — Art. 5(2) GDPR

**NIS2 article (verbatim, Art. 20(1) sentence 1 + Art. 21(2)(a) + Art. 21(2)(f)):**
> "Member States shall ensure that the management bodies of essential and important entities approve the cybersecurity risk-management measures taken by those entities in order to comply with Article 21, oversee their implementation and can be held liable for infringements by the entities of that Article." — Art. 20(1) sentence 1 NIS 2 (per SR-NIS2-032/033/034)
> "[Member States shall ensure that essential and important entities take appropriate and proportionate technical, operational and organisational measures to … ] (a) policies on risk analysis and information system security; … (f) policies and procedures to assess the effectiveness of cybersecurity risk-management measures." — Art. 21(2)(a)+(f) NIS 2

**Scope comparison:**
- GDPR scope: controller-side data-protection policies (Art. 24) + DPO designation (Art. 37-39).
- NIS 2 scope: management-body approve/oversee/liable (Art. 20(1)) + policies on risk analysis + IS security + effectiveness-assessment (Art. 21(2)(a)+(f)).
- Scope overlap: **Y (when the entity is both a controller and an NSIE).**

**Trigger comparison:**
- GDPR trigger: controller's adoption of data-protection policies.
- NIS 2 trigger: management-body approve + entity adoption of risk-analysis + IS-security + effectiveness-assessment policies.
- Trigger alignment: **PARTIAL** (both policy-driven but different governance hooks).

**Threshold/timeline comparison:**
- GDPR: no quantitative cadence.
- NIS 2: no quantitative cadence (entity-discretionary under Recital 56).
- Tension: **NONE**.

**Recipient comparison:**
- GDPR: data subject, DPA.
- NIS 2: management body, competent authority.

**Content template comparison:**
- GDPR: not codified for content.
- NIS 2: not codified for content.

**Classified relationship (from CRDA):** COMPLEMENTARY
**Verified relationship (after OJ-text analysis):** **SAME — COMPLEMENTARY**
**Reasoning:** GDPR mandates the policy artefact (data-protection policies); NIS 2 mandates the governance act (management body approves/oversees/liable) AND the policy artefact. NIS 2's governance architecture (approve/oversee/liable) extends to the GDPR policies for entities that are both controllers and NSIEs.

**Scope-disjoint test:** Does this pair actually overlap on a single event at a single party? **Y (when the controller-NSIE adopts data-protection policies subject to management-body approval)**. No reclassification required.

**Downstream implication (Phase 1C Doc 07):** Phase 2 obligation derivation must produce a layered stack: GDPR Art. 24 data-protection policies + NIS 2 Art. 20(1) management-body approve/oversee/liable governance + NIS 2 Art. 21(2)(a)+(f) policy artefacts.

---

##### Pair: GDPR ↔ CRA

**GDPR article (verbatim, Art. 24(1) + Art. 5(2)):**
> [as quoted above]

**CRA article (verbatim, Art. 24(1) + Art. 13(8) + Art. 27(1)/(2)/(5)/(8)/(9)):**
> "Open-source software stewards shall put in place and document in a verifiable manner a cybersecurity policy to foster the development of a secure product with digital elements as well as an effective handling of vulnerabilities by the developers of that product." — Art. 24(1) CRA (per SR-CRA-072)
> "Products with digital elements and the processes put in place by the manufacturer shall be presumed to be in conformity with the essential cybersecurity requirements set out in Part I of Annex I where those products and processes conform to harmonised standards …" — Art. 27(1) CRA
> "The Commission may adopt implementing acts establishing common specifications …" — Art. 27(2) CRA
> "Common specifications … shall be presumed to be in conformity with the essential cybersecurity requirements set out in Part I of Annex I …" — Art. 27(5) CRA
> "A product with digital elements and the processes put in place by the manufacturer for which the manufacturer has obtained an EU statement of conformity or an EU certificate under a European cybersecurity certification scheme adopted pursuant to Regulation (EU) 2019/881 … shall be presumed to be in conformity with the essential cybersecurity requirements …" — Art. 27(8) CRA

**Scope comparison:**
- GDPR scope: controller-side data-protection policies.
- CRA scope: manufacturer-side cybersecurity policies + OSS steward policies + presumption-of-conformity machinery.
- Scope overlap: **N (typically)** — different actors (controller vs. manufacturer/steward).

**Trigger comparison:**
- GDPR trigger: controller's adoption of data-protection policies.
- CRA trigger: manufacturer/steward adoption of cybersecurity policies.
- Trigger alignment: **DIFFERENT**.

**Threshold/timeline comparison:**
- GDPR: no quantitative cadence.
- CRA: support period + 10-year Annex II accessibility.
- Tension: **NONE**.

**Recipient comparison:**
- GDPR: data subject, DPA.
- CRA: MSA.

**Content template comparison:**
- GDPR: not codified for content.
- CRA: not codified for content (Art. 24(1) OSS steward policy contains 4 sub-elements (i)–(iv) per SR-CRA-072).

**Classified relationship (from CRDA):** COMPLEMENTARY
**Verified relationship (after OJ-text analysis):** **SAME — COMPLEMENTARY**
**Reasoning:** GDPR addresses controller policies for personal data processing; CRA addresses manufacturer policies for product cybersecurity (and OSS steward policies under Art. 24). The two policies are different artefacts and can coexist without overlap.

**Scope-disjoint test:** Does this pair actually overlap on a single event at a single party? **Conditional (Y when the controller is also a manufacturer or OSS steward; N otherwise)**. No reclassification required.

**Downstream implication (Phase 1C Doc 07):** Phase 2 obligation derivation must produce two parallel tracks: GDPR Art. 24 controller policies + CRA Art. 24/27 manufacturer/steward policies + presumption-of-conformity machinery.

---

##### Pair: GDPR ↔ DORA

**GDPR article (verbatim, Art. 24(1) + Art. 5(2)):**
> [as quoted above]

**DORA article (verbatim, Art. 5(2) + Art. 6(1) + Art. 6(4)):**
> "The management body of the financial entity shall define, approve, oversee and be responsible for the implementation of all arrangements related to the ICT risk management framework." — Art. 5(2) DORA (per SR-DORA-026)
> "Financial entities shall have a sound, comprehensive and well-documented ICT risk management framework … which enables them to address ICT risk quickly, efficiently and comprehensively." — Art. 6(1) DORA (per SR-DORA-027)
> "Financial entities shall assign the responsibility for managing and overseeing ICT risk to a control function and ensure an appropriate level of independence … in accordance with the three lines of defence model, or an internal risk management and control model." — Art. 6(4) DORA (per SR-DORA-028)

**Scope comparison:**
- GDPR scope: controller-side data-protection policies.
- DORA scope: financial-entity ICT risk-management framework + three-lines-of-defence.
- Scope overlap: **Y (when a DORA financial entity is a controller processing personal data).**

**Trigger comparison:**
- GDPR trigger: controller's adoption of data-protection policies.
- DORA trigger: management-body define/approve/oversee/implement + ICT risk-management framework.
- Trigger alignment: **PARTIAL** (both policy-driven).

**Threshold/timeline comparison:**
- GDPR: no quantitative cadence.
- DORA: no quantitative cadence (entity-discretionary under Art. 4 proportionality).
- Tension: **NONE**.

**Recipient comparison:**
- GDPR: data subject, DPA.
- DORA: management body, NCA.

**Content template comparison:**
- GDPR: not codified for content.
- DORA: Art. 6(2) lists 5 artefact types — `strategies, policies, procedures, ICT protocols and tools`.

**Classified relationship (from CRDA):** COMPLEMENTARY
**Verified relationship (after OJ-text analysis):** **SAME — COMPLEMENTARY**
**Reasoning:** DORA's ICT risk-management framework is the dominant authority for financial entities; GDPR policies become a module within the framework. DORA Art. 5(2) governance + GDPR Art. 5(2) accountability stack.

**Scope-disjoint test:** Does this pair actually overlap on a single event at a single party? **Y (when the DORA financial entity is a controller adopting data-protection policies within the framework)**. No reclassification required.

**Downstream implication (Phase 1C Doc 07):** Phase 2 obligation derivation must produce a DORA-led stack: DORA Art. 6(1) ICT risk-management framework as carrier + GDPR Art. 24 data-protection policies as module.
