### Chapter V synthesis

#### Counts

| Category | Instances | Clauses affected |
|---|---|---|
| VAG | 11 | 10 / 10 |
| POLY | 7 | 7 / 10 |
| COORD | 5 | 5 / 10 |
| SCOPE-Q | 2 | 2 / 10 |
| **Total** | **25** | **10 / 10** |

| Severity | Instances |
|---|---|
| S1 — Low | 0 |
| S2 — Medium | 14 |
| S3 — High | 11 |

#### Recurring patterns

- `appropriate` and `adequate` appear in 5 of 10 clauses — the Chapter's twin Berry-flagged qualifiers.
- `compelling legitimate interests` (Art. 49) propagates from Art. 21(1) — cross-chapter POLY.
- Schrems II is the dominant interpretive anchor for Chapter V but is non-textual.

#### Cross-clause structural observations

1. **Art. 44 is the umbrella principle.** All transfers (Art. 45–49) must comply with Chapter V conditions AND with Art. 44's `not undermined` outcome obligation.
2. **Art. 45–46 are the primary mechanisms.** Adequacy (Art. 45) and safeguards (Art. 46) cover the majority of legitimate transfers. Art. 49 derogations are narrow last-resort exceptions.
3. **Schrems II decision cascade.** The Schrems II (C-311/18) ruling invalidated Privacy Shield, requiring SCCs to be supplemented with case-by-case transfer impact assessments. This non-textual standard shapes Chapter V application.
4. **`essentially equivalent` test vs `appropriate safeguards` standard.** Schrems II uses `essentially equivalent` for adequacy assessment; Art. 46(1) uses `appropriate safeguards`. The relationship between the two standards is judicially established but textually ambiguous.
5. **Art. 49(1)(a) consent three-way inconsistency.** Art. 4(11) `unambiguous` + Art. 9(2)(a) `explicit` + Art. 49(1)(a) `explicitly` — three different consent qualifiers for three different consent scenarios. Layer 2 would resolve.

#### Anti-pattern notes for synthesis

- Chapter V is the only chapter whose compliance depends on non-OJ-text anchors (Schrems II). The drafter's intent is sometimes inferred from CJEU jurisprudence rather than from the regulation text.
- Cross-regulation: DORA Art. 28(8) ICT third-party risk management overlaps with GDPR Chapter V for financial entities transferring data to non-EU ICT providers.

## CRA

#### 3.1 CRA-CL01 — Art. 6 chapeau + (a) — Essential-requirements gate (Part I)

**Source locus:**

> Products with digital elements shall be made available on the market only where:
> **(a)** they meet the **<mark>essential cybersecurity requirements set out in Part I of Annex I</mark>** …;

**Instance 1 — VAG — S2 — `essential cybersecurity requirements`**

Inherits from Annex I Part I (analysed in `09_CRA_AnnexI_EssentialRequirements.md`). The `essential` modifier is itself undefined.

- **R1:** `essential` = mandatory (all Part I requirements must be met).
- **R2:** `essential` = core (some Part I requirements apply, depending on risk assessment).
- **R3:** `essential` = minimum baseline (additional requirements allowed).

| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | All | In-Directive: Art. 13(3) `shall indicate whether and, if so in what manner, the security requirements set out in Part I, point (2), of Annex I are applicable` |
| R2 | Conditional | In-Directive: Art. 13(3) implies Part I (2) is conditional on risk assessment |
| R3 | Baseline | None in OJ |

R2 is the literal reading (Part I (1) is mandatory; Part I (2) is conditional). S2.

*Berry anchor:* §3.3.1 (`essential`).

**Instance 2 — POLY — S2 — `meet`**

`meet` — what threshold?

- **R1:** Pass conformity assessment (Art. 32).
- **R2:** Self-declare conformity.
- **R3:** Demonstrate by technical documentation.

| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Third-party verified | None in OJ; Art. 32(1)(a) module A (internal) |
| R2 | Self-declared | In-Directive: Art. 32(1)(a) internal control |
| R3 | Documented | In-Directive: Art. 13(12) technical documentation |

R1/R2/R3 are class-dependent (Art. 32 routes products to modules). S2.

*Berry anchor:* §3.3.1 (`meet`).

**Instance 3 — VAG — S2 — `shall be made available on the market only where`**

The "only where" is a **necessary condition** reading. The reading:

- **R1:** Strict necessary condition (no market access without Annex I conformity).
- **R2:** Necessary condition with carve-outs (e.g. trade fairs per Art. 4(2)).
- **R3:** Necessary condition with delegation to implementing acts.

| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Strict | In-Directive: literal `only where` |
| R2 | With Art. 4(2) carve-outs | In-Directive: Art. 4(2) trade-fair presentation |
| R3 | With IA carve-outs | None in OJ |

R1 is the literal reading; R2 is the harmonised reading. S2.

*Berry anchor:* §5.1 (`only where`).

### 4. Cross-clause structural observations (Art. 6–8)

1. **Art. 6 is the essential-requirements gate.** The two-part structure (Part I for product properties; Part II for processes) mirrors GDPR's two-tier obligation (Art. 25 design + Art. 32 security). The installation-condition proviso (CL02) is CRA-unique and operationally consequential: a non-conforming product that is "properly installed" satisfies Art. 6(a).

2. **Art. 7's two criteria (CL06, CL07) define important products.** The criteria are disjunctive (criterion A OR B). Three materially distinct readings admit materially different compliance populations. The Commission's 11 December 2025 implementing acts (CL09) will refine the criteria.

3. **Art. 8's certification requirement (CL10) is the most consequential single clause in this article.** Critical products require European cybersecurity certification at assurance level "substantial." As of mid-2025, **no European cybersecurity certification scheme has been adopted under the CSA that covers critical-product categories.** The fallback (CL12) routes products to Art. 32(3) conformity assessment (modules B+C or H).

4. **The Art. 7/8/Alex III/IV architecture is a 3-tier classification** (analogous to AI Act's risk tiers):
   - Default (Art. 6): all products meet Annex I (essential requirements) — Annex I compliance route.
   - Important (Art. 7): Class I or Class II per Annex III — Art. 32(2)/(3) conformity assessment.
   - Critical (Art. 8): Annex IV — European cybersecurity certification at substantial level (or Art. 32(3) fallback).

   Each tier imposes progressively stricter conformity-assessment obligations.

5. **`core functionality` (CL04) is the single most consequential classification term.** The reading determines which products fall into Class I/II vs default. R1–R3 admit materially different compliance populations.

6. **Annex III/IV are flagged for Layer 2.** Their ambiguity is definitional regress ("core functionality of a product category") rather than Berry linguistic in the strict sense.

---

**End of Art. 6–8 file. Next: `03_CRA_Art13_Manufacturers.md` (the heart of CRA).**

## CRA v0.2 — Article 13 Obligations of Manufacturers (THE HEART)

> **Source.** OJ L, 20.11.2024, p. 34–37. `03_REFERENCE_MATERIAL/Regulatory_References/CRA.txt`, lines ~2177–2370.
>
> **Pattern.** Article quoted in full (paragraph by paragraph), then per-clause analysis. Universal `[recital NN]` convention applied.
>
> **Article 13 is the operational core of CRA** — 25 paragraphs, ~35 atomic clauses. It is the analog of GDPR Art. 32 and NIS 2 Art. 21. It defines the 6-phase product-lifecycle obligations (planning, design, development, production, delivery, maintenance), the cybersecurity risk assessment, the due-diligence duty on third-party components, the support period, the technical documentation, and the corrective-measures duty.

---

### 1. Article 13 — full text (key paragraphs)

> **Article 13 — Obligations of manufacturers**
>
> **1.** When placing a product with digital elements on the market, manufacturers shall ensure that it has been designed, developed and produced in accordance with the essential cybersecurity requirements set out in Part I of Annex I.

> **2.** For the purpose of complying with paragraph 1, manufacturers shall undertake an assessment of the cybersecurity risks associated with a product with digital elements and take the outcome of that assessment into account during the **planning, design, development, production, delivery and maintenance phases** of the product with digital elements with a view to minimising cybersecurity risks, preventing incidents and minimising their impact, **including in relation to the health and safety of users**.

> **3.** The cybersecurity risk assessment shall be documented and updated as appropriate during a support period to be determined in accordance with paragraph 8 of this Article. That cybersecurity risk assessment shall comprise at least an analysis of cybersecurity risks based on the **intended purpose and reasonably foreseeable use, as well as the conditions of use, of the product with digital elements**, such as the operational environment or the assets to be protected, taking into account the length of time the product is expected to be in use. The cybersecurity risk assessment shall indicate whether and, if so in what manner, the security requirements set out in Part I, point (2), of Annex I are applicable to the relevant product with digital elements and how those requirements are implemented as informed by the cybersecurity risk assessment. It shall also indicate how the manufacturer is to apply Part I, point (1), of Annex I and the vulnerability handling requirements set out in Part II of Annex I.

> **4.** When placing a product with digital elements on the market, the manufacturer shall include the cybersecurity risk assessment referred to in paragraph 3 of this Article in the technical documentation required pursuant to Article 31 and Annex VII. …

> **5.** For the purpose of complying with paragraph 1, manufacturers shall **exercise due diligence when integrating components sourced from third parties** so that those components do not compromise the cybersecurity of the product with digital elements, including when integrating components of free and open-source software that have not been made available on the market in the course of a commercial activity.

> **6.** Manufacturers shall, upon identifying a vulnerability in a component, including in an open source-component, which is integrated in the product with digital elements report the vulnerability to the person or entity manufacturing or maintaining the component, and **address and remediate** the vulnerability in accordance with the vulnerability handling requirements set out in Part II of Annex I. …

> **7.** The manufacturers shall systematically document, in a manner that is proportionate to the nature and the cybersecurity risks, **relevant cybersecurity aspects** concerning the products with digital elements, including vulnerabilities of which they become aware and any relevant information provided by third parties, and shall, where applicable, update the cybersecurity risk assessment of the products.

> **8.** Manufacturers shall ensure, when placing a product with digital elements on the market, and for the support period, that vulnerabilities of that product, including its components, are handled effectively and in accordance with the essential cybersecurity requirements set out in Part II of Annex I.
>
> Manufacturers shall determine the support period so that it reflects the length of time during which the product is expected to be in use, taking into account, in particular, **reasonable user expectations, the nature of the product, including its intended purpose**, as well as **relevant Union law determining the lifetime** of products with digital elements. When determining the support period, manufacturers may also take into account the support periods of products with digital elements offering a similar functionality placed on the market by other manufacturers, the availability of the operating environment, the support periods of integrated components that provide core functions and are sourced from third parties as well as relevant guidance provided by the dedicated administrative cooperation group (ADCO) established pursuant to Article 52(15) and the Commission. **The matters to be taken into account in order to determine the support period shall be considered in a manner that ensures proportionality.**
>
> Without prejudice to the second subparagraph, the support period shall be **at least five years**. Where the product with digital elements is expected to be in use for less than five years, the support period shall correspond to the expected use time.
>
> …
>
> **9.** Manufacturers shall ensure that each security update, as referred to in Part II, point (8), of Annex I, which has been made available to users during the support period, **remains available after it has been issued for a minimum of 10 years or for the remainder of the support period, whichever is longer**.

> **10.** Where a manufacturer has placed subsequent substantially modified versions of a software product on the market, that manufacturer may ensure compliance with the essential cybersecurity requirement set out in Part II, point (2), of Annex I only for the version that it has last placed on the market, provided that theusers of the versions that were previously placed on the market have access to the version last placed on the market free of charge and do not incur additional costs to adjust the hardware and software environment in which they use the original version of that product.

> **11.** Manufacturers may maintain public software archives enhancing user access to historical versions. In those cases, users shall be clearly informed in an easily accessible manner about risks associated with using unsupported software.

> **12.** Before placing a product with digital elements on the market, manufacturers shall draw up the technical documentation referred to in Article 31. They shall carry out the chosen conformity assessment procedures as referred to in Article 32 or have them carried out. Where compliance … has been demonstrated by that conformity assessment procedure, manufacturers shall draw up the EU declaration of conformity in accordance with Article 28 and affix the CE marking in accordance with Article 30.

> **13.** Manufacturers shall **keep the technical documentation and the EU declaration of conformity at the disposal of the market surveillance authorities for at least 10 years** after the product with digital elements has been placed on the market or for the support period, whichever is longer.

> **14.** Manufacturers shall ensure that procedures are in place for products with digital elements that are part of a series of production to remain in conformity with this Regulation. …

> **15.** Manufacturers shall ensure that their products with digital elements bear a **type, batch or serial number or other element allowing their identification** …

> **16.** Manufacturers shall indicate the **name, registered trade name or registered trademark of the manufacturer, and the postal address, email address or other digital contact details**, as well as, where applicable, the website where the manufacturer can be contacted, on the product with digital elements …

> **17.** For the purposes of this Regulation, manufacturers shall designate a **single point of contact** to enable users to communicate directly and rapidly with them, including in order to facilitate reporting on vulnerabilities of the product with digital elements. …

> **18.** Manufacturers shall ensure that products with digital elements are accompanied by the **information and instructions to the user set out in Annex II**, in paper or electronic form. …

> **19.** Manufacturers shall ensure that the **end date of the support period** referred to in paragraph 8, including at least the month and the year, is clearly and understandably specified at the time of purchase …

> **20.** Manufacturers shall either provide a copy of the EU declaration of conformity or a **simplified EU declaration of conformity** with the product with digital elements. …

> **21.** From the placing on the market and for the support period, manufacturers who know or have reason to believe that the product with digital elements or the processes put in place by the manufacturer are not in conformity with the essential cybersecurity requirements set out in Annex I shall **immediately take the corrective measures** necessary to bring that product with digital elements or the manufacturer's processes into conformity, or to withdraw or recall the product, as appropriate.

> **22.** Manufacturers shall, upon a reasoned request from a market surveillance authority, provide that authority, in a language which can be easily understood by that authority, with all the information and documentation, in paper or electronic form, necessary to demonstrate the conformity …

> **23.** A manufacturer that ceases its operations and, as a result, is not able to comply with this Regulation shall inform, before the cessation of operations takes effect, the relevant market surveillance authorities as well as, by any means available and to the extent possible, the users of the relevant products with digital elements placed on the market, of the impending cessation of operations.

> **24.** The Commission may, by means of implementing acts taking into account European or international standards and best practices, specify the format and elements of the **software bill of materials** referred to in Part II, point (1), of Annex I. …

> **25.** In order to assess the dependence of Member States and of the Union as a whole on software components and in particular on components qualifying as free and open-source software, ADCO may decide to conduct a Union wide **dependency assessment** for specific categories of products with digital elements. …

---

#### 3.1 CRA-CL14 — Art. 13(1) — Design/development/production duty

**Source locus:**

> When placing a product with digital elements on the market, manufacturers shall ensure that it has been **designed, developed and produced** in accordance with the essential cybersecurity requirements set out in Part I of Annex I.

**Instance 1 — COORD — S2 — `designed AND developed AND produced`**

The 3-phase coordination. Three readings:
- **R1:** Three distinct phases (each must comply).
- **R2:** Composite description (one process).
- **R3:** Each independently required.

| R3 | Independent | None in OJ |

R1 is the literal reading. S2.

*Berry anchor:* §5.5 (three-way coordination).

Inherits Art. 3(21) definition: `first making available of a product with digital elements on the Union market`. Same POLY as Art. 3(21) D21.

When determining the support period, manufacturers may also take into account the support periods of products with digital elements offering a similar functionality placed on the market by other manufacturers, the availability of the operating environment, the support periods of integrated components that provide core functions and are sourced from third parties as well as relevant guidance provided by the dedicated administrative cooperation group (ADCO) … The matters to be taken into account in order to determine the support period shall be considered in a manner that ensures proportionality.

### 4. Cross-clause structural observations (Art. 14)

1. **Art. 14 has a dual-track architecture:** Track A = actively exploited vulnerabilities (¶1–2, 24h/72h/14d); Track B = severe incidents (¶3–4, 24h/72h/1m). The two tracks have identical temporal tiers but different clock starts (14d vs 1m). This is structurally parallel to GDPR Art. 33 (controller notification) vs Art. 34 (data-subject communication).

2. **`severe incident` is the definitional regress.** Art. 14(5) defines it via `sensitive or important data or functions` (CL61) and `malicious code` (CL62). Both terms are undefined. Same pattern as NIS 2 Art. 23(3) `significant incident` and DORA Art. 18 `major ICT-related incident`.

3. **`becomes aware of` (CL51, CL56) is the cross-regulation awareness-trigger pattern.** Same as NIS 2 Art. 23(4)(a)(b) and GDPR Art. 33(1). National implementations have begun diverging on when awareness attaches (R1 actual vs R2 constructive knowledge).

4. **`simultaneously` (CL51, CL56) is CRA-unique.** No other regulation requires simultaneous notification to two bodies (CSIRT + ENISA). NIS 2 requires notification to the CSIRT only (or competent authority). GDPR requires notification to the supervisory authority only.

5. **`structured, machine-readable format that is easily automatically processable` (CL65) is CRA-unique.** No other regulation in the corpus requires machine-readable user notification. NIS 2 Art. 23(4)(a)(b) uses the same temporal pattern but does not specify format.

6. **T3-vs-text gaps in Art. 14:** T3 had CRA-C25 (Art. 14(1) reporting) and CRA-C26 (Art. 14(3) reporting). The v0.2 expands these into 18 atomic clauses (CL51–CL68). T3's coverage was thin (2 clauses for 10 paragraphs). v0.2 captures the full article.

---

#### 2.1 CRA-CL101 — Art. 24(1) — OSS steward cybersecurity policy

**Source locus:**

> Open-source software stewards shall put in place and document in a verifiable manner a cybersecurity policy to foster the development of a secure product with digital elements as well as an effective handling of vulnerabilities by the developers of that product. That policy shall also foster the voluntary reporting of vulnerabilities as laid down in Article 15 by the developers of that product and take into account the specific nature of the open-source software steward and the legal and organisational arrangements to which it is subject. That policy shall, in particular, include aspects related to documenting, addressing and remediating vulnerabilities and promote the sharing of information concerning discovered vulnerabilities within the open-source community.

**Instance 1 — VAG — S3 — `foster the development of a secure product`**

`foster` — encourage? require? enable?

- **R1:** Encourage (soft obligation).
- **R2:** Require (hard obligation).
- **R3:** Enable (provide means).

| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Encourage | In-Directive: literal `foster` |
| R2 | Require | None in OJ |
| R3 | Enable | None in OJ |

R1 is the literal reading. R2/R3 produce materially distinct compliance populations. S3.

**Instance 2 — VAG — S3 — `document in a verifiable manner`**

`verifiable manner` — verified by whom?

- **R1:** Self-verification (internal audit).
- **R2:** Third-party verification.
- **R3:** MSA verification.

Same Berry POLY as Art. 3(20) `support period` definition. Remediation vs mitigation.

## CRA v0.2 — Articles 27–32 Conformity

> **Source.** OJ L, 20.11.2024, p. 46–50. `03_REFERENCE_MATERIAL/Regulatory_References/CRA.txt`, lines ~2878–3121.
>
> **Pattern.** Articles quoted in full, then per-clause analysis.
>
> **Articles 27–32 define the conformity machinery:** Art. 27 (presumption of conformity via harmonised standards/common specs/certification), Art. 28 (EU declaration), Art. 30 (CE marking), Art. 31 (technical documentation), Art. 32 (conformity assessment procedures — modules A/B+C/H).

---

### 1. Atomic clause map

| Clause ID | Locus | Type | Highest Sev. | Gist |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| CRA-CL108 | Art. 27(1) (presumption — harmonised standards) | Presumption | **S2** | Products conforming to harmonised standards are presumed compliant. |
| CRA-CL109 | Art. 27(2) (common specifications) | Common specs | **S2** | Commission may adopt implementing acts establishing common specifications. |
| CRA-CL110 | Art. 27(5) (common-specs presumption) | Presumption | **S2** | Products conforming to common specs are presumed compliant. |
| CRA-CL111 | Art. 27(8) (certification presumption) | Certification | **S2** | Products with EU statement/certificate under CSA presumed compliant. |
| CRA-CL112 | Art. 27(9) (delegated acts — certification schemes) | Delegated acts | **S2** | Commission specifies which CSA schemes demonstrate conformity. |
| CRA-CL113 | Art. 28(1) (EU declaration content) | Declaration | **S2** | EU declaration states fulfilment of Annex I requirements. |
| CRA-CL114 | Art. 28(2) (declaration format) | Format | **S2** | Declaration follows Annex V model; simplified follows Annex VI. |
| CRA-CL115 | Art. 28(3) (single declaration) | Single declaration | **S2** | Single EU declaration for products under multiple Union acts. |
| CRA-CL116 | Art. 28(4) (manufacturer responsibility) | Responsibility | **S2** | By drawing up declaration, manufacturer assumes responsibility for compliance. |
| CRA-CL117 | Art. 30(1) (CE marking affixing) | CE marking | **S2** | CE marking affixed visibly, legibly, indelibly; for software, on declaration or website. |
| CRA-CL118 | Art. 30(3) (CE marking timing) | Timing | **S2** | CE marking affixed before placing on market. |
| CRA-CL119 | Art. 30(6) (labels/pictograms) | Labels | **S2** | Commission may adopt implementing acts for labels/pictograms related to security. |
| CRA-CL120 | Art. 31(1) (technical documentation content) | Tech docs | **S2** | Tech docs contain all data/details demonstrating Annex I compliance; at least Annex VII. |

### 2. Detailed cards (selected)

## CRA v0.2 — Annex I Essential Cybersecurity Requirements

> **Source.** OJ L, 20.11.2024, p. 68–69. `03_REFERENCE_MATERIAL/Regulatory_References/CRA.txt`, lines ~4153–4234.
>
> **Pattern.** Each Part quoted verbatim with highlighting, then per-clause analysis. Universal `[recital NN]` convention.
>
> **Annex I is the binding normative core of CRA.** Part I defines security properties for products; Part II defines vulnerability-handling obligations for manufacturers. These are referenced by Art. 6, 7, 8, 13, 27, 28, 32, and are the ultimate compliance target. 21 of the 26 T2 clauses lived in Annex I.

---

### 1. Annex I — full text

## NIS2

#### 3.1 NIS2-CL01 — Art. 20(1) sentence 1 — Three-verb governance + liability

**Source locus:**

> Member States shall ensure that the management bodies of essential and important entities **<mark>approve the cybersecurity risk-management measures taken by those entities in order to comply with Article 21, oversee its implementation and can be held liable for infringements by the entities of that Article</mark>**.

**Instance 1 — VAG + POLY — S2 — `cybersecurity risk-management measures`**

The object of the three coordinated verbs is the same phrase as in Art. 21(1) opening. The Directive does not define `cybersecurity risk-management measures`; the term is composed of:

- `cybersecurity` (Art. 6(3), cross-referenced to Reg. 2019/881)
- `risk-management measures` (composite; no definition in Art. 6)
- `measures` (no definition; cf. Art. 21(1) `technical, operational and organisational measures`)

| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | `measures` = the 10-item list of Art. 21(2)(a)–(j) | In-Directive: Art. 21(2) chapeau `shall include at least the following` (illustrative floor) |
| R2 | `measures` = whatever the entity adopts in fulfilment of Art. 21(1) opening | In-Directive: Art. 21(1) `appropriate and proportionate` suggests entity-discretionary |
| R3 | `measures` = implementing acts under Art. 21(5) (sectoral technical/methodological specs) | In-Directive: Art. 21(5) `implementing acts laying down the technical and the methodological requirements` |

R1 and R2 produce materially distinct obligations (R1 admits checklist-compliance; R2 requires entity-specific risk management). R3 is conditional on implementing acts adopted by 17 October 2024. S2.

*Berry anchor:* §3.3.1 (`measures`).

**Instance 2 — VAG — S2 — `in order to comply with Article 21`**

The purpose clause `in order to comply with Article 21` may be:

- **R1:** Purpose-indicative (explains why the measures are approved).
- **R2:** Purpose-restrictive (the approval obligation is bounded by Art. 21's scope).

| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Indicative | None in OJ |
| R2 | Restrictive | None in OJ; recital 75 (management responsibility) |

R1 is the dominant reading. R2 is rejected in standard EU-drafting convention. Layer 1 catalogues the surface; the reading converges in practice.

*Berry anchor:* §5.1 (`in order to`).

**Instance 3 — COORD + POLY — S2 — three-verb coordination**

The sentence coordinates three verbs: `approve … oversee … can be held liable`.

Berry §5.5 parallelism test: the verbs are coordinated by AND (`approve, oversee and can be held liable`), with no explicit nested structure. Three readings:

| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Three distinct obligations (each is independently required) | In-Directive: recital 75 implies distinct |
| R2 | One composite oversight duty (approve + oversee + liable together constitute the oversight) | None in OJ |
| R3 | Two distinct obligations (approve + oversee) and one consequence (held liable) | None in OJ |

R1 and R3 produce operationally distinct compliance paths (R1 admits piecemeal performance: "we approved but did not oversee"; R3 binds the acts of approval and oversight together). S2.

*Berry anchor:* §5.5 (three-way coordination).

**Instance 4 — VAG + POLY — S2 — `can be held liable for infringements`**

- `held liable` — civil, criminal, administrative, or all three? The Directive does not specify.
- `infringements` — which type? Infringements of Art. 21(1) (the opening) or Art. 21(2) (the list) or Art. 23 (reporting)?

| # | Reading (liability type) | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Civil liability (management body sued by entity or shareholders) | None in OJ; recital 75 |
| R2 | Criminal liability (prosecution) | None in OJ; recital 75 |
| R3 | Administrative liability (fines, disqualification) | None in OJ; recital 75 |
| R4 | All three (the Directive is silent; Member States decide) | None in OJ; recital 75 |

R4 is the dominant reading (the carve-out of Art. 20(1) sentence 2 explicitly defers to national law for public institutions, suggesting Member States otherwise have full latitude). The reading converges; the surface ambiguity is genuine.

| # | Reading (infringement scope) | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Infringements of Art. 21(1) opening | In-Directive: literal `of that Article` |
| R2 | Infringements of any Art. 21 sub-paragraph | In-Directive: purposive reading |
| R3 | Infringements of Art. 21 + Art. 23 (reporting) | None in OJ; recital 75 |

R1 is the literal reading; R2 is the dominant purposive reading; R3 imports reporting obligations by analogy. S2 because the difference is between which paragraphs trigger personal liability.

*Berry anchor:* §5.1 (`liable`, `infringements`).

---

#### 3.2 NIS2-CL02 — Art. 20(1) sentence 2 — Public-institution carve-out

**Source locus:**

> The application of this paragraph shall be **without prejudice to national law as regards the liability rules applicable to public institutions, as well as the liability of public servants and elected or appointed officials**.

**Instance 1 — SCOPE-Q — S2 — `public institutions`**

The carve-out applies to `public institutions`. The term is undefined in NIS 2. The reading:

- **R1:** `public institutions` = the entities designated as `public administration entities` in Art. 6(35).
- **R2:** `public institutions` = any entity owned or controlled by the State, broader than Art. 6(35).
- **R3:** `public institutions` = constitutional bodies (parliaments, central banks, judiciary).

| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Art. 6(35) `public administration entity` | In-Directive: Art. 6(35) is the closest definition |
| R2 | Broader (any State-controlled entity) | None in OJ |
| R3 | Narrower (constitutional bodies only) | In-Directive: Art. 6(35) expressly excludes judiciary, parliaments, central banks |

R1 and R3 are the operative readings. R1 is broad (excludes constitutional bodies per Art. 6(35) chapeau); R3 is narrow (constitutional bodies are exempt from Art. 6(35) and thus from the Art. 20(1) carve-out's relevance). The reading affects whether state-owned enterprises (which are Art. 6(35)-excluded by the industrial/commercial-character test) are inside or outside the carve-out. S2.

*Berry anchor:* §5.1 (`public institutions` undefined).

**Instance 2 — VAG — S2 — `elected or appointed officials`**

The carve-out covers `elected or appointed officials`. The reading:

- **R1:** Officials elected or appointed to public office (politicians, ministers).
- **R2:** Officials appointed to positions of authority within public institutions (civil servants, agency heads).
- **R3:** Both.

| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Elected officials only | None in OJ |
| R2 | Appointed officials only | None in OJ |
| R3 | Both | In-Directive: literal `elected or appointed` is inclusive OR |

R3 is the literal reading. S2 because the difference is between who is in the carve-out (R3 = all senior public officials; R1 = politicians only).

*Berry anchor:* §5.4.7 (OR).

**Layer 1 observation** — The Art. 20(1) carve-out defers to **national law** for the liability rules. This is the clearest single instance in NIS 2 of **Directive-vs-Regulation cross-border divergence potential**: a Dutch public-entity board member and a German public-entity board member face different liability exposures despite identical NIS 2 obligations. Flagged for Layer 2.

---

#### 3.3 NIS2-CL03 — Art. 20(2) sentence 1 — Mandatory management-body training

**Source locus:**

> Member States shall ensure that the **<u>members of the management bodies</u>** of essential and important entities **are required to follow training**.

**Instance 1 — VAG — S3 — `are required to follow training`**

`required to follow training` — required by whom? Member States (the obligation target of the sentence is Member States) or the entity itself? Three materially distinct readings:

- **R1:** Member States must legislate a statutory training requirement for management-body members.
- **R2:** Member States must ensure the entity itself requires its management-body members to follow training.
- **R3:** Both (Member States must legislate AND ensure the entity requires it).

| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Statutory training requirement | In-Directive: literal `Member States shall ensure` is directed to MS, not entities |
| R2 | Entity-internal requirement | In-Directive: literal `are required to follow` is directed to members |
| R3 | Both | In-Directive: standard MS-implementation pattern |

R1 and R2 yield materially different compliance obligations (R1 is a national-law requirement; R2 is an entity-internal governance requirement). S3.

*Berry anchor:* §5.1 (`required`).

**Instance 2 — VAG — S2 — `training`**

Polysemy inherited from the cybersecurity-vocabulary ecosystem. Three readings:

- **R1:** Formal certified curriculum (e.g. ISO/IEC 27001 lead-auditor, CISSP, CISM).
- **R2:** Awareness briefing (annual cybersecurity overview).
- **R3:** Tabletop exercise (incident simulation).

| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Formal curriculum | None in OJ; ENISA training guidance |
| R2 | Awareness briefing | None in OJ; recital 50 (cyber hygiene) |
| R3 | Tabletop exercise | None in OJ |

Same polysemy as GDPR C27/C28 (training in data-protection context). The variant readings converge in practice (entities do all three) but the Directive does not specify which is required.

*Berry anchor:* §3.3.1 (`training`).

**Instance 3 — SCOPE-Q — S2 — `members of the management bodies`**

The Directive says `members` (universal). The reading:

- **R1:** Every individual member of every management body.
- **R2:** The management body as a collective (i.e. some members must be trained).
- **R3:** A designated cybersecurity-knowledgeable subset.

| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Every member | In-Directive: literal `members` (plural) |
| R2 | At least one member (collective) | None in OJ |
| R3 | Designated subset | None in OJ |

R1 is the literal reading. R2 and R3 are operational concessions. S2 because the difference is between training-100% and training-a-subset.

*Berry anchor:* §5.2.1 (`members`).

---

#### 3.4 NIS2-CL04 — Art. 20(2) sentence 1 — Purpose clause

**Source locus (continued):**

> …in order that they gain **<mark>sufficient knowledge and skills</mark>** to enable them to **identify risks and assess cybersecurity risk-management practices and their impact on the services provided by the entity**.

**Instance 1 — VAG — S2 — `sufficient knowledge and skills`**

`sufficient` — sufficient for what? Sufficient to perform the management-body function generally, or sufficient to perform the cybersecurity-governance function specifically?

- **R1:** General-management sufficiency.
- **R2:** Cybersecurity-specific sufficiency.

| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | General management | None in OJ; recital 50 |
| R2 | Cybersecurity-specific | In-Directive: literal `cybersecurity risk-management practices` qualifier |

R2 is the dominant reading. S2 because the difference affects training scope.

*Berry anchor:* §5.1 (`sufficient`).

**Instance 2 — VAG — S2 — `identify risks and assess cybersecurity risk-management practices`**

`identify risks` — risks to what? Risks to the entity? Risks to recipients of services? Risks to other entities?

- **R1:** Risks to the entity.
- **R2:** Risks to recipients of services.
- **R3:** Both.

| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Entity only | In-Directive: literal context |
| R2 | Recipients only | None in OJ |
| R3 | Both | In-Directive: Art. 21(1) covers `incidents on recipients of their services` |

R1 and R3 are operative; R2 is rejected by Art. 21(1) context. S2.

*Berry anchor:* §3.3.1 (`risks`).

**Instance 3 — COORD — S2 — `identify risks AND assess cybersecurity risk-management practices AND their impact on the services provided by the entity`**

Three coordinated activities. Three readings:

- **R1:** All three must be performed (cumulative).
- **R2:** Two distinct activities (risk identification + impact assessment) bundled.
- **R3:** Single activity: assess risk-management practices including their impact.

| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Three distinct | In-Directive: literal `identify … and assess … and their impact` |
| R2 | Two distinct | None in OJ |
| R3 | One composite | None in OJ |

R1 is the literal reading. S2.

*Berry anchor:* §5.5 (three-way coordination).

---

#### 3.6 NIS2-CL06 — Art. 20 chapeau — Implicit proportionality / scope hook

**Source locus (chapeau of Art. 20, paraphrased from sentence 1):**

> Member States shall ensure that [the obligations] apply to essential and important entities.

**Instance 1 — VAG + POLY — S2 — `essential and important entities`**

The term is defined in Art. 3 but Art. 3's own classification criteria (size thresholds, sectoral lists, Member State discretion) are open. Three layers of indeterminacy:

- Layer A: `essential` vs. `important` — different supervisory regimes (Art. 32 fines, Art. 33 enforcement measures).
- Layer B: size thresholds (medium vs. large per Recommendation 2003/361/EC).
- Layer C: sectoral coverage (Annex I/II).

| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Two-tier binary (essential OR important) | In-Directive: Art. 3(1)–(2) |
| R2 | Member-State-discretionary boundary | In-Directive: Art. 3(1)(e), (g) — Member States may identify additional essential entities |
| R3 | Annex I/II sector lists are exhaustive | In-Directive: Art. 2(1) + Annex I/II |

R1 is the structural reading. R2 is the Member-State-discretion reading. R3 is the sectoral-exhaustive reading. S2 because the surface ambiguity drives whether an entity falls in the scope of Art. 20 (R1/R2/R3 may give different answers for borderline cases like cloud-computing providers with mixed sectoral exposure).

*Berry anchor:* §3.3.1 (`essential and important entities`); §5.2.1 (Annex lists).

**Layer 1 observation** — The Art. 3 classification of an entity is a **prerequisite** for all of Art. 20, 21, 22, 23, 24, 25 obligations. If an entity is not classified as essential or important, none of these apply. The classification itself is ambiguous (R2: Member-State discretion; R3: Annex I/II exhaustive). This is the **single most important scope-defining layer of NIS 2** — flagged in synthesis.

---

### 4. Cross-clause structural observations (Art. 20)

1. **Art. 20(1) is the only direct personal-liability provision in NIS 2.** No other article in Chapter IV imposes liability on natural persons. The provision is short but materially consequential. National implementations have begun diverging (e.g. Belgium's transposition law, France's transposition law) on which liability types attach.
2. **Art. 20(2) creates a two-tier training obligation:** mandatory (for members) + encouraged (for employees). The asymmetry is intentional in the Directive; the operational impact is that entities may be judged on management-body training compliance but not on employee training.
3. **The management-body training obligation is the second cross-regulation `training` polysemy hotspot** (the first was GDPR Art. 39 DPO training; the third is CRA Annex I Part II §2(c) `security update procedures` which includes training). All three share the same POLY pattern.
4. **The `essential and important entities` classification propagates through every Chapter IV clause.** Resolving it is the prerequisite for any other finding.

---

### 5. T3-to-v0.2 mapping

| T3 clause | T3 article | v0.2 clause | v0.2 article | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| NIS2-C01 | Art. 20(1) | NIS2-CL01 + NIS2-CL02 | Art. 20(1) | v0.2 splits sentence 1 (CL01) from sentence 2 carve-out (CL02); T3 only analysed sentence 1. |
| NIS2-C02 | Art. 20(2) | NIS2-CL03 + NIS2-CL04 + NIS2-CL05 | Art. 20(2) | v0.2 splits the three coordinated sub-phrases; T3 treated sentence 2 as a single clause. |
| (none) | — | NIS2-CL06 | Art. 20 chapeau | v0.2 adds the Art. 3 classification-propagation analysis that T3 omitted. |

#### 3.1 NIS2-CL07 — Art. 21(1) sentence 1 — Risk-management core obligation

**Source locus:**

> Member States shall ensure that essential and important entities take **<mark>appropriate and proportionate technical, operational and organisational measures</mark>** to manage the risks posed to the security of network and information systems which those entities use for their operations or for the provision of their services, and to prevent or minimise the **impact** of incidents on recipients of their services and on other services.

**Instance 1 — VAG — S3 — `appropriate and proportionate`**

The opening qualifier. Three materially distinct readings:

- **R1:** `appropriate` and `proportionate` are independent (entity must satisfy both tests, separately).
- **R2:** `appropriate` modifies `proportionate` (entity must satisfy proportionality, with `appropriate` as a placeholder synonym).
- **R3:** `appropriate and proportionate` is a single compound qualifier (a fixed phrase).

| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Independent | None in OJ |
| R2 | Hierarchical | None in OJ |
| R3 | Compound | In-Directive: Art. 21(1) sentence 2 operationalises proportionality by specific factors (exposure, size, likelihood, severity) |

R1 and R3 produce materially distinct compliance obligations (R1 admits two separate failure modes; R3 admits one composite test). S3.

This is the dominant VAG trigger of NIS 2. **It propagates to all 10 sub-points of Art. 21(2)(a)–(j) and to Art. 21(3) and Art. 21(4).** Every measure listed in Art. 21(2) inherits the `appropriate and proportionate` qualifier by reference to "The measures referred to in paragraph 1".

*Berry anchor:* §5.1 (`appropriate`, `proportionate`).

**Instance 2 — POLY — S2 — `technical, operational and organisational measures`**

Three-term coordination. Three readings:

- **R1:** Three distinct categories of measures (entity must implement at least one of each).
- **R2:** Cumulative description (a measure is "technical, operational and organisational" if it has all three characteristics).
- **R3:** Open list (entity may add more categories).

| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Distinct categories | In-Directive: literal `technical, operational and organisational` |
| R2 | Cumulative single measure | None in OJ |
| R3 | Open list | In-Directive: recital 56 (proportionality) |

R1 is the literal reading. R3 is the dominant reading (recital 56 implies entity-discretion on additional categories). S2.

*Berry anchor:* §5.5 (three-way coordination).

**Instance 3 — VAG + POLY — S2 — `manage the risks`**

`manage the risks` — what counts as `management`? Reading:

- **R1:** Identify + assess + mitigate.
- **R2:** Treat (avoid, accept, transfer, mitigate).
- **R3:** Continuous monitoring + adjustment.

| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Identify-assess-mitigate | None in OJ; ISO/IEC 27005 |
| R2 | ISO 27005 risk-treatment | None in OJ; ISO/IEC 27005 |
| R3 | Continuous monitoring | None in OJ; NIST RMF |

*Berry anchor:* §3.3.1 (`manage`).

**Instance 4 — POLY — S2 — `impact`**

`impact` — on recipients of services (downstream effect) or on other services (cascading effect)?

- **R1:** Impact on direct recipients.
- **R2:** Impact on other services (cascade).
- **R3:** Both.

| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Recipients | In-Directive: literal `recipients of their services` |
| R2 | Other services | In-Directive: literal `on other services` |
| R3 | Both | In-Directive: literal `recipients … and … other services` |

R3 is the literal reading (coordinated by `and`). The fact that the two objects are coordinated by AND rather than OR means the entity must consider impact on both. S2 because the surface ambiguity is between "focus on direct recipients" and "consider cascading effects".

*Berry anchor:* §3.3.1 (`impact`); §5.4.7 (AND).

---

#### 3.2 NIS2-CL08 — Art. 21(1) sentence 2 — Risk-management factors

**Source locus:**

> Taking into account **<mark>the state-of-the-art and, where applicable, relevant European and international standards, as well as the cost of implementation</mark>**, the measures referred to in the first subparagraph shall ensure a level of security of network and information systems **appropriate to the risks posed**. When assessing the proportionality of those measures, due account shall be taken of **the degree of the entity's exposure to risks, the entity's size and the likelihood of occurrence of incidents and their severity, including their societal and economic impact**.

**Instance 1 — VAG — S3 — `state-of-the-art`**

Same Berry-classic vague term as GDPR Art. 25(1) and Art. 32(1). Three materially distinct readings:

- **R1:** `state-of-the-art` = current best practice (qualitative).
- **R2:** `state-of-the-art` = published standards (e.g. ISO/IEC 27001:2022, NIST CSF 2.0).
- **R3:** `state-of-the-art` = cutting-edge research (academic / frontier).

| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Best practice | In-Directive: `state-of-the-art and … standards` (R1 contrasts with R2) |
| R2 | Standards | In-Directive: same as R1 |
| R3 | Frontier | None in OJ |

R1 and R2 produce materially distinct obligations (R1 admits "industry-typical" measures; R2 mandates standards-based measures). R3 is rejected by recital 56 (cost-of-implementation consideration implies off-the-shelf). S3.

*Berry anchor:* §5.1 (`state-of-the-art`).

**Instance 2 — VAG — S2 — `cost of implementation`**

`cost` — to whom? The entity's cost? The national-economy cost? The supply-chain cost?

- **R1:** Entity's cost.
- **R2:** National-economy cost (recital 56).
- **R3:** Supply-chain cost (R3 imports the Art. 21(2)(d) supply-chain frame).

| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Entity's cost | In-Directive: literal `cost of implementation` (the implementation is the entity's) |
| R2 | National-economy cost | In-Directive: recital 56 |
| R3 | Supply-chain cost | None in OJ |

R1 is the literal reading. R2 is a non-operational reading (national-economy cost is not assessable by the entity). R3 is T3-imposed (no support in the OJ text). S2.

*Berry anchor:* §3.3.1 (`cost`).

**Instance 3 — VAG — S2 — `appropriate to the risks posed`**

`appropriate to the risks posed` — what is the proportionality function?

- **R1:** Linear (proportional to exposure × likelihood × impact).
- **R2:** Tiered (mapped to entity's classification under Art. 3).
- **R3:** Risk-tiered (mapped to specific risk-assessment output).

| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Linear | None in OJ |
| R2 | Tiered | None in OJ; Art. 3 essential/important classification |
| R3 | Risk-tiered | In-Directive: Art. 21(1) sentence 2 lists risk factors |

R3 is the dominant reading. S2.

*Berry anchor:* §5.1 (`appropriate to the risks`).

**Instance 4 — VAG — S2 — `the degree of the entity's exposure to risks`**

`exposure` — exposure to threat? Exposure to impact? Exposure to incident likelihood?

- **R1:** Threat exposure.
- **R2:** Impact exposure (downstream).
- **R3:** Combined.

| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|

| R3 | Combined | None in OJ; Art. 6(9) `risk` (loss or disruption) |

**Instance 5 — VAG — S2 — `the entity's size`**

`size` — by employee count, by turnover, by balance sheet total, by user count? The Recommendation 2003/361/EC definition is the standard EU benchmark, but the Directive does not reference it.

- **R1:** Employee count.
- **R2:** Turnover.
- **R3:** Balance sheet total.

R1–R3 are the standard EU micro/small/medium/large measures. R4 is T3-imposable. S2.

**Instance 6 — VAG — S2 — `likelihood of occurrence of incidents and their severity`**

`likelihood` + `severity` (AND). The two-factor risk-treatment.

- `likelihood` — same POLY as in Art. 6(9) (frequentist / Bayesian / qualitative).
- `severity` — same VAG as `severe` in Art. 23(3) (defines `significant incident`).

#### 3.3 NIS2-CL09 — Art. 21(2) chapeau — All-hazards approach + physical environment

**Source locus:**

> The measures referred to in paragraph 1 shall be **based on an all-hazards approach** that aims to protect network and information systems and **<mark>the physical environment of those systems</mark>** from incidents…

**Instance 1 — VAG — S2 — `all-hazards approach`**

`all-hazards` — natural, technological, malicious, accidental? Cybersecurity only or broader?

- **R1:** All cybersecurity hazards (intentional, accidental, environmental).
- **R2:** All hazards including natural disasters (earthquake, flood), pandemics.
- **R3:** All hazards including physical security (intrusion, theft, sabotage).

| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Cybersecurity-only | None in OJ |
| R2 | Including natural | None in OJ |
| R3 | Including physical | In-Directive: literal `physical environment of those systems` |

R3 is the dominant reading (the chapeau explicitly includes physical-environment protection). S2.

*Berry anchor:* §5.1 (`all-hazards`).

**Instance 2 — VAG + SCOPE-Q — S2 — `the physical environment of those systems`**

The physical environment is in scope. The reading:

- **R1:** Server rooms, data centres, network equipment closets.
- **R2:** Buildings (offices, call centres).
- **R3:** Surrounding critical infrastructure (power, cooling, telecoms).

| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Data-centre scope | In-Directive: literal `physical environment` |
| R2 | Building scope | None in OJ |
| R3 | Critical-infrastructure scope | None in OJ |

R1 is the literal reading. R2 and R3 are operational extensions. S2.

**Layer 1 observation** — The physical-environment scope of NIS 2 is broader than DORA, CRA, AI Act (all of which are software/cyber-focused). The all-hazards approach with physical-environment coverage is the **single most distinctive feature of NIS 2 vs the other four regulations** in the corpus. **For software-compliance analysis, the physical-environment dimension is out-of-scope**: it does not generate software requirements, only physical-security requirements. The clause is included for completeness but flagged as **out-of-scope for the software-compliance track of the methodology**. Layer 3 (inference-trust) will need to adjudicate whether software-software integration controls are needed for the physical-environment dimension (e.g. environmental sensors feeding into SIEM).

*Berry anchor:* §5.1 (`physical environment`); §5.2.1 (scope).

`inter alia` — among other things. The reporting is non-exhaustive; the entity may report more than the listed items. Standard EU drafting; the cross-border-impact info is mandatory, but other info is permitted.

---

### 4. Cross-clause structural observations (Art. 24)

1. **Art. 24 is the only NIS 2 provision allowing MS to require certification.** This is the integration point with the Cybersecurity Act (Reg. 2019/881). The Directive is permissive (`may require`) but the Commission's delegated acts (Art. 24(2)) can convert permissibility into specificity.

2. **The `may require` + `shall encourage` asymmetry is deliberate.** The certification hook is permissive (Member State discretion); the trust services hook is encouragement-only (entity discretion). The asymmetry reflects the regulatory philosophy: certification is a market-instrument, trust services are a service-quality instrument.

3. **`insufficient levels of cybersecurity` is the trigger for delegated acts.** Same definitional regress pattern as Art. 23(3) `significant incident`: the trigger is itself undefined, leaving Commission discretion to specify. This is structurally identical to the DORA Art. 26(11) TLPT normative-tension (binding obligation anchored to non-binding TIBER-EU).

4. **No direct entity-facing obligation in Art. 24 itself.** The entity's obligation is indirect: if the MS requires certification (Art. 24(1)) and the Commission specifies (Art. 24(2)), the entity must comply with the certification requirement to demonstrate Art. 21 compliance.

---

### 5. T3-to-v0.2 mapping

T3 had no clause for Art. 24 — it was outside the T3 cybersecurity-taxonomy subset. The v0.2 expansion adds Art. 24 with 4 atomic clauses (CL48–CL51).

| T3 clause | T3 article | v0.2 clause | v0.2 article | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| (none) | — | NIS2-CL48 | Art. 24(1) sentence 1 | T3 omitted; v0.2 adds. |
| (none) | — | NIS2-CL49 | Art. 24(1) sentence 2 | T3 omitted; v0.2 adds. |
| (none) | — | NIS2-CL50 | Art. 24(2) | T3 omitted; v0.2 adds. |
| (none) | — | NIS2-CL51 | Art. 24(3) | T3 omitted; v0.2 adds. |

## DORA

## DORA v0.2 — Chapter II Section I + Art. 4: Governance and Proportionality (Art. 4–6)

> **Source.** OJ L 333, 27.12.2022, p. 29–32. `03_REFERENCE_MATERIAL/Regulatory_References/DORA.txt`, lines **1635–1789**.
>
> **Pattern.** Each article quoted in full, then per-clause analysis. Universal `[recital NN]` convention. DORA-specific fields: `definitional-anchor`, `proportionality-status: Art. 4 applies`, `RTS-pending`, `scope`.
>
> **Why these three articles together (umbrella file `02a`).** Art. 4 is in Ch. I (General Provisions) but the proportionality principle it establishes **propagates to every clause in Ch. II–V Sec. I**. Art. 5 (governance) and Art. 6 (framework chapeau) are the structural anchors to which Art. 4 attaches. Grouping them in `02a` lets the reader see the **proportionality overlay + management-body liability + framework chapeau** as a single conceptual block. The remaining 10 articles of Ch. II Sec. II (Art. 7–16, the operational risk-management provisions) are analysed in `02b_DORA_Ch2Sec2_Framework.md`.
>
> **Articles in this file:** 3 articles, 17 paragraphs (Art. 4: 3; Art. 5: 4 + 9 sub-points; Art. 6: 10 + 8 sub-points), 13 atomic clauses (CL4-1 … CL6-5).

---

### 1. Articles covered

| Article | Title | Paragraphs | T3 clause | New in v0.2? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Art. 4 | Proportionality principle | 3 | **C01** (proportionality) | No (extended — T3 had a single clause; v0.2 has 3) |
| Art. 5 | Governance and organisation | 4 + 9 sub-points (a)–(i) | **C02** (governance), **C03** (management body), **C04** (board training) | No (extended — T3 had 3 clauses; v0.2 has 5) |
| Art. 6 | ICT risk management framework (chapeau) | 10 + 8 sub-points (a)–(h) | **C05** (framework chapeau), **C06** (control function), **C07** (audit), **C08** (resilience strategy) | No (extended — T3 had 4 clauses; v0.2 has 5) |

**Note on Art. 4 (Proportionality):** the three paragraphs of Art. 4 attach proportionality to (i) Ch. II as the default; (ii) Ch. III, IV, V Sec. I by specific reference; (iii) competent-authority review under Art. 6(5) and Art. 16(2). Each clause card in the rest of v0.2 carries a `proportionality-status: Art. 4 applies` flag (per pilot convention).

**Note on definitional anchors:** every ICT-related clause in this file inherits from DORA Art. 3(2) → NIS 2 Art. 6(1) `network and information system`; Art. 3(5) `ICT risk`; Art. 3(30) `management body` (composite cross-reference, see pilot file §2.10). The Art. 3(22) `critical or important function` (S3 tautology, see pilot file §2.7) propagates to Art. 6(8)(f).

**Note on RTS-pending:** no RTS mandate in this article range. Art. 15 (RTS mandate to ESAs on Art. 9–11 elements) is meta-procedural; the substantive impact lives in Art. 9–11, not in this file. The Art. 4 proportionality principle is **self-implementing** — no RTS/ITS specifies the criteria.

---

### 3. Verbatim OJ text — Art. 4–6

#### 4.4 CL5-1 — Art. 5(1) — Internal governance and control framework chapeau

**Source locus:** Art. 5(1).

> Financial entities shall have in place an **internal governance and control framework** that ensures an **effective and prudent** management of ICT risk, in accordance with Article 6(4), in order to achieve a high level of digital operational resilience.

**Instance 1 — VAG — S3 — `internal governance and control framework`**

The framework chapeau. Three materially distinct readings:

- **R1:** Three lines of defence model (per Art. 6(4) cross-reference).
- **R2:** Two lines of defence (business + control, common in some Member States).
- **R3:** Composite: any recognised internal-control model.

| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Three lines of defence | In-Directive: Art. 6(4) `according to the three lines of defence model` |
| R2 | Two lines of defence | None in OJ |
| R3 | Any model | In-Directive: Art. 6(4) `or an internal risk management and control model` |

R3 is the literal reading (Art. 6(4) gives the option). S3 (the choice between R1 and R3 yields materially distinct control populations).

*Berry anchor:* §3.3.1 (`governance and control framework`); §5.4.7 (AND/OR structure).

**Instance 2 — VAG — S3 — `effective and prudent`**

Two vague qualifiers (AND). Both are Berry classics.

- **R1:** Effective (outcome-based) AND prudent (process-based, conservative).
- **R2:** Hendiadys (single concept: risk-management discipline).
- **R3:** Either, per entity judgment.

| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Distinct qualifiers | In-Directive: literal `and` |
| R2 | Hendiadys | None in OJ |
| R3 | Either | None in OJ |

R1 is the literal reading. R1 vs R3 admit materially distinct compliance populations (R1 requires both outcome and process; R3 allows either). S3.

*Berry anchor:* §5.1 (`effective`, `prudent`); §5.4.7 (AND); cf. CRA-CL27 `handled effectively` (same pattern).

**Instance 3 — POLY — S2 — `a high level of digital operational resilience`**

The target standard. Inherits DORA-D01 `digital operational resilience` (S3 `build, assure and review`).

- **R1:** All three lifecycle phases per R1 of DORA-D01.
- **R2:** Continuous process per R2 of DORA-D01.
- **R3:** All three control families per R3 of DORA-D01.

| R1 | Lifecycle phases | In-Directive: DORA-D01 |

#### 4.5 CL5-2 — Art. 5(2) chapeau — Management body 4-verb coordination

**Source locus:** Art. 5(2), first subparagraph.

> The management body of the financial entity shall **define, approve, oversee and be responsible for** the implementation of all arrangements related to the ICT risk management framework referred to in Article 6(1).

**Instance 1 — COORD — S3 — `define, approve, oversee and be responsible for` (4-verb coordination)**

The **longest management-body coordination pattern in Ch. II**. Four verbs (AND) attached to the management body. Three materially distinct readings:

- **R1:** Four discrete actions (define a framework; approve it; oversee its execution; bear responsibility for outcomes).
- **R2:** Composite (a single management role covering all four aspects).
- **R3:** Two pairs: (define + approve = strategic), (oversee + responsible for = operational).

| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Four discrete | In-Directive: literal four-verb coordination |
| R2 | Composite | None in OJ |
| R3 | Two pairs | None in OJ |

R1 is the literal reading. R1 vs R2 admit materially distinct compliance populations (R1: management body must produce four distinct artifacts/activities; R2: one composite activity suffices). S3.

*Berry anchor:* §5.4.7 (four-way coordination); §5.5 (verb parallelism); cf. pilot §2.1 (DORA-D01 `build, assure and review` — three verbs, same pattern).

**Instance 2 — VAG — S3 — `the management body`**

Inherits DORA-D30 `management body` (composite cross-reference stitching 5 sectoral definitions + national-law residual; see pilot file §2.10). The two readings:

- **R1:** Executive (running the entity day-to-day).
- **R2:** Supervisory (oversight, non-executive).
- **R3:** Both (per composite definition).

| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |

#### 4.6 CL5-3 — Art. 5(2)(a)–(d) — Sub-points: responsibility + standards + roles + strategy

**Source locus:** Art. 5(2), sub-points (a)–(d).

> (a) bear the ultimate responsibility for managing the financial entity's ICT risk;
>
> (b) put in place policies that aim to ensure the maintenance of **high standards of availability, authenticity, integrity and confidentiality, of data**;

- **R3:** Top-of-hierarchy responsibility (board-level, not operational).

#### 4.11 CL6-3 — Art. 6(4)+(6) — Independence + 3 lines of defence + internal audit

**Source locus:** Art. 6(4) and 6(6).

> 4. Financial entities, other than microenterprises, shall assign the responsibility for managing and overseeing ICT risk to a control function and ensure an **appropriate level of independence of such control function** in order to avoid conflicts of interest. Financial entities shall ensure **appropriate segregation and independence of ICT risk management functions, control functions, and internal audit functions, according to the three lines of defence model, or an internal risk management and control model**.
>
> 6. The ICT risk management framework of financial entities, other than microenterprises, shall be subject to **internal audit by auditors on a regular basis in line with the financial entities' audit plan**. Those auditors shall possess **sufficient knowledge, skills and expertise in ICT risk, as well as appropriate independence**. The frequency and focus of ICT audits shall be commensurate to the ICT risk of the financial entity.

**Instance 1 — VAG — S3 — `appropriate level of independence of such control function`**

The independence threshold for the ICT-risk control function. Three materially distinct readings:

- **R1:** Structural independence (organisational separation; reporting line outside the ICT function).

#### 6.1 Art. 4 propagation pattern — three distinct proportionality regimes

Art. 4 establishes **three distinct proportionality regimes** that operate simultaneously:

- **Art. 4(1) — Ch. II proportionality** (CL4-1): default for the ICT risk management framework.
- **Art. 4(2) — Ch. III, IV, V Sec. I proportionality** (CL4-2): default for incident reporting, testing, and third-party risk.
- **Art. 4(3) — CA-review proportionality** (CL4-3): meta-provision requiring CAs to consider proportionality when reviewing the framework.

The three regimes are **not symmetric**: Art. 4(1) is self-implementing (the entity applies it directly); Art. 4(2) defers to chapter-specific clauses (Art. 16 simplification, Art. 25(3) microenterprise exception); Art. 4(3) is addressed to CAs.

- **Berry anchor:** §3.3.1 (`principle of proportionality`); §5.1 (`proportionate`).
- **Structural implication:** the proportionality overlay is **inherited by every Ch. II–V Sec. I clause** in v0.2. Every clause card in `02b`–`05a` carries `proportionality-status: Art. 4 applies`. The v0.2 convention was established in the pilot file (`01_DORA_Art3_Definitions.md` §2 note on Art. 4).
- **Cross-regulation consistency:** Art. 4's proportionality pattern mirrors **NIS 2 Art. 1(2)** (proportionality in scope) and **CRA Art. 1(2)** (proportionality for microenterprises and SMEs). DORA Art. 4 is more elaborate than either, providing explicit CA-review (CL4-3) that NIS 2 and CRA do not have.

#### 6.2 Art. 5(2) — the 4-verb coordination as DORA's governance signature

Art. 5(2) uses the **4-verb coordination `define, approve, oversee and be responsible for`** to describe the management body's role. This is the **longest management-body coordination pattern in DORA** and the operational analog of:

- **DORA-D01 `build, assure and review`** (3-verb, definition of digital operational resilience; pilot file §2.1)
- **CRA Art. 13(1) `designed, developed and produced`** (3-verb, manufacturer duty; CRA-CL14)
- **GDPR Art. 5(1)(a)–(f) principles** (6 principles, no verb coordination; analogous to a list of duties)

The 4-verb coordination is **structurally distinct** from the 3-verb patterns in DORA-D01 and CRA-CL14 because it adds the `be responsible for` element — the **non-delegable accountability** anchor. The first three verbs (`define, approve, oversee`) describe **processes**; the fourth (`be responsible for`) describes the **outcome liability**.

- **Berry anchor:** §5.4.7 (4-way coordination); §5.5 (verb parallelism); §3.3.1 (`responsible for`).
- **Structural implication:** the 4-verb pattern is the **signature DORA governance clause**. It propagates to Art. 6(1) (framework chapeau — `sound, comprehensive and well-documented` is the framework's qualities, mirroring the management body's four duties), to Art. 5(2)(a) (`ultimate responsibility`), to Art. 5(2)(d) (`overall responsibility for setting and approving the digital operational resilience strategy`), and to Art. 6(10) (`fully responsible for the verification of compliance`).
- **Cross-regulation consistency:** the 4-verb pattern is **unique to DORA** in the 5-regulation corpus. NIS 2 Art. 20 (governance) uses single-verb obligations (`approve`, `oversee`). CRA Art. 13(1) uses 3 verbs. GDPR Art. 24 (controller responsibility) uses `demonstrate compliance` (single verb). AI Act Art. 14 (human oversight) uses 4 verbs but in a different configuration. DORA's 4-verb pattern is the most elaborate.

#### 6.3 Art. 6 chapeau — the framework's three-attribute triad

Art. 6(1) opens with `sound, comprehensive and well-documented` (three attributes, AND). This is the **framework-level analog** of:

- **Art. 24(1) `sound and comprehensive`** (testing programme, two attributes; CL24-1)
- **CRA Annex I Part I §1 chapeau** `products with digital elements shall be designed, developed and produced …` (manufacturing lifecycle, three verbs; CRA-CL14)
- **NIS 2 Art. 21(1) `appropriate and proportionate technical, operational and organisational measures`** (measures chapeau)

The 3-attribute triad (`sound, comprehensive and well-documented`) is **the most demanding framework chapeau in the 5-regulation corpus** — it requires three distinct qualities (effectiveness, completeness, traceability). CRA Annex I Part I §1 has three verbs (design, develop, produce) but they describe **processes** not qualities. DORA's triad describes **qualities of an artifact** (the framework itself), which is a different kind of obligation.

- **Berry anchor:** §5.1 (`sound`, `comprehensive`, `well-documented`); §5.4.7 (AND); §5.5 (parallelism).
- **Structural implication:** the framework's three attributes set the bar for every Ch. II–V Sec. I clause. Any clause that fails one of the three (e.g. a framework that is `comprehensive and well-documented` but not `sound`) would be non-compliant.
- **Cross-regulation consistency:** the triad is the most explicit framework-quality specification in the corpus. NIS 2 Art. 21(1) uses `appropriate and proportionate` (2 qualifiers); CRA Annex I Part I uses process verbs; GDPR Art. 24 uses `appropriate` (1 qualifier). DORA's 3-attribute triad is the most demanding.

#### 6.4 Art. 5(2)(a) and Art. 6(10) — non-delegable responsibility

Two clauses establish **non-delegable responsibility** of the management body / financial entity:

- **Art. 5(2)(a)** (CL5-3 Instance 1): `bear the ultimate responsibility for managing the financial entity's ICT risk`.
- **Art. 6(10)** (CL6-5 Instance 7): `the financial entity remains fully responsible for the verification of compliance with the ICT risk management requirements`.

The pattern is the **non-delegable accountability principle** — the management body cannot outsource the responsibility, even if it outsources the tasks. The principle is a **case-law concept** from banking supervision (Basel Committee) and EU financial-services law (CRD Art. 13, MiFID Art. 9 governance obligations).

- **Berry anchor:** §3.3.1 (`ultimate`, `fully`); §5.1 (`remains`).
- **Structural implication:** the non-delegable responsibility propagates to **out-sourcing** (Art. 28), **intra-group services** (Art. 28(4)), and **ICT third-party contracts** (Art. 30). A financial entity cannot escape Art. 5–6 obligations by contracting them out; the management body remains the accountable party.
- **Cross-regulation consistency:** the non-delegable responsibility is the **DORA-specific implementation** of a general EU financial-services law principle. NIS 2 has no equivalent (essential/important entities are not "governed" in the same way). CRA has no equivalent (manufacturers may delegate conformity assessment to notified bodies). GDPR Art. 24 (controller responsibility) has the same principle for data protection.

#### 6.5 Art. 6(4) — the 3-lines-of-defence option

Art. 6(4) gives financial entities **two control-model options** (OR): the **three lines of defence** model (business → risk management → internal audit) OR **any internal risk management and control model**. The 3-lines-of-defence model is the **banking-supervision standard** (Basel Committee, EBA Guidelines on Internal Governance), but DORA Art. 6(4) explicitly **opens the door to alternative models** (e.g. COSO, ISO 31000, or proprietary models).

- **Berry anchor:** §3.3.1 (`three lines of defence`); §5.4.7 (OR).
- **Structural implication:** the option is **non-trivial** because the 3-lines-of-defence model implies three distinct functions with separate reporting lines; alternative models may have two (business + control) or four (split internal audit from compliance). The model choice affects the segregation requirement in CL6-3 Instance 1-3.
- **Cross-regulation consistency:** the 3-lines-of-defence model is **specific to EU financial-services law**. NIS 2 has no equivalent. CRA has no equivalent. The closest analog is **CRD IV Art. 74** (internal governance arrangements) which uses similar language.

## DORA v0.2 — Chapter II Section II: ICT Risk Management Framework (Art. 7–16)

> **Source.** OJ L 333, 27.12.2022, p. 31–39. `03_REFERENCE_MATERIAL/Regulatory_References/DORA.txt`, lines **1791–2272**.
>
> **Pattern.** Each article quoted in full, then per-clause analysis. Universal `[recital NN]` convention. DORA-specific fields: `definitional-anchor: DORA Art. 3(2) → NIS 2 Art. 6(1)`, `proportionality-status: Art. 4 applies`, `RTS-pending: Art. 15` (where applicable).
>
> **Chapter II Section II is the operational heart of DORA.** Art. 7–9 establish the *Identify–Protect–Detect* baseline (NIST CSF mapping). Art. 10–11 establish *Detect–Respond*. Art. 12 establishes *Recover* (backup). Art. 13 closes the loop with *Learn–Evolve*. Art. 14 mandates crisis communication. Art. 15 is the meta-procedural RTS mandate to ESAs further specifying Art. 9–11. Art. 16 is the **simplified framework for exempted entities** (small investment firms, exempted payment institutions, exempted e-money institutions, small IORPs) — structurally parallel to Art. 9(4) but lowered in operational granularity.
>
> **Articles in this file:** 10 articles (Art. 7–16), ~50 operative paragraphs, **26 atomic clauses** (CL7-1 … CL16-2). Compacted from v0.2-pre (34 clauses) per user direction: D1=compact heavily, D5=entity-facing + Q1=software architecture + reporting deadlines, D3=Layer 1 only (no Layer 2 normative force introduced). Variant readings consolidated to 2 where 2 of 3 readings converged to materially similar compliance practice.

---

### 1. Articles covered

| Article | Title | Paragraphs | Cross-references | Compacted clauses |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Art. 7 | ICT systems, protocols and tools | chapeau + 4 sub-points (a)–(d) | Art. 4 proportionality | **CL7-1** |
| Art. 8 | Identification | 7 paragraphs | Art. 6(1) framework, Art. 3(5)/(8) ICT risk and incident | **CL8-1, CL8-2, CL8-3** |
| Art. 9 | Protection and prevention | 4 paragraphs; **9(4) is the HINGE** with 6 sub-points (a)–(f) | Art. 3(2) network and information system, Art. 4 proportionality, Art. 6(1) | **CL9-1, CL9-2, CL9-3, CL9-4** |
| Art. 10 | Detection | 4 paragraphs | Art. 17, Art. 25 testing | **CL10-1, CL10-2, CL10-3** |
| Art. 11 | Response and recovery | 11 paragraphs | Art. 6(1), Art. 8, Art. 12, Art. 14, Art. 19, Art. 25 | **CL11-1, CL11-2, CL11-3, CL11-4** |
| Art. 12 | Backup policies and procedures, restoration and recovery procedures and methods | 7 paragraphs | Art. 3(2) network and information system | **CL12-1, CL12-2, CL12-3, CL12-4** |
| Art. 13 | Learning and evolving | 7 paragraphs | Art. 6(8), Art. 11, Art. 25, Art. 30(2)(i) | **CL13-1, CL13-2** |
| Art. 14 | Communication | 3 paragraphs | Art. 6(1) | **CL14-1, CL14-2** |
| Art. 15 | Further harmonisation of ICT risk management tools, methods, processes and policies | chapeau + 7 sub-points (a)–(g) | Art. 9(2), Art. 9(4)(c), Art. 10, Art. 11, Art. 6(5) | **CL15-1** |
| Art. 16 | Simplified ICT risk management framework | 3 paragraphs; **16(1) second sub-paragraph** = 8 sub-points (a)–(h) parallel to Art. 9(4) | Art. 5–15 (exemption scope) | **CL16-1, CL16-2** |

**Note on Art. 4 (Proportionality):** every operative clause card in this file carries `proportionality-status: Art. 4 applies` (per pilot convention). Art. 4 propagates to Ch. II Sec. II clauses; Art. 16(1) second sub-paragraph explicitly references `size and overall risk profile` (Art. 16(3) RTS preamble) and is itself the **proportionality-output clause** for exempted entities.

**Note on Art. 15 (RTS-pending):** Art. 15 mandates ESAs to develop draft RTS by **17 January 2024** (the deadline has passed; as of mid-2025, RTS adopting acts are either adopted or in the final adoption pipeline — **Delegated Regulation (EU) 2024/1774** of 31 May 2024 supplements DORA with the regulatory technical standards for Art. 15(a)–(g), adopted by the Commission on the basis of ESA Joint Committee draft). The RTS mandate covers 7 areas: Art. 9(2) ICT security policies (sub-point a); Art. 9(4)(c) access-control rights (sub-point b); Art. 10(1)+(2) detection mechanisms (sub-point c); Art. 11(1) ICT business continuity policy (sub-point d); Art. 11(6) BCP testing (sub-point e); Art. 11(3) ICT response and recovery plans (sub-point f); Art. 6(5) ICT risk management framework review report (sub-point g). Every flagged instance in this file tagged `RTS-pending: Art. 15` is materially constrained by the eventual RTS content.

**Note on definitional anchors:** every ICT-related clause in Ch. II Sec. II inherits from **DORA Art. 3(2) → NIS 2 Art. 6(1)** (`network and information system`). Clauses hinging on `critical or important function` inherit from **DORA-D22** (the tautological definition flagged at pilot file §2.7; **S3 regress**).

**Compaction note (D1 / D5 / D3):** the compaction consolidates atomic clauses where two or more sub-clauses address the same operational concern (e.g. Art. 11(1)+(2) chapeau+objectives → CL11-1; Art. 11(7)+(8)+(9) crisis+records+CSD → CL11-3). Variant readings are consolidated to 2 (instead of 3) where 2 of 3 readings yield materially similar compliance obligations — `[no third reading identified]` is flagged explicitly. The full Art. 9(4) hinge is preserved at full density (15 instances within one card) because the 6 sub-points (a)–(f) are the operational core of DORA's protection regime. **D3 invariant: no Layer 2 normative force introduced** — the catalogue remains Layer 1 (ambiguity description); the resolution/adjudication is deferred to Layer 2 (cross-regulation synthesis) and to the entity (P7).

---
